" A Scorrisu labourer was engaged in the grounds of an asylum, near Edinburgh, and received in- structions to pay no attention whatever to the remarks of tho patients who noticed him. Some little time after the doctor, looking at the progress of the work, mildly suggested an alteration. The workman dug steadily on, and never lifted his head, The doctor raised his voice, the man dug energeti- cally. The doctor threatened, stormed, and finally thundered out, "Do you know who lam ?" The son of the soil straightened his back, looked at him for a minute, and shaking his head sorrowfully, ex- claimed, " Puir deleerious cratur, I'm sorry for ye," and went on calmly with his work. LADIESI LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE. 4. —9-t4frifir; In general, the cut of co is is longer, a inh y ihave rounded basques sloped away in front like a man's morning coat.'. A tight-fitting, plain habit 'bodice, with square short basque some four inches ibelgEr the waist, with leather belt and square 'buckle round the waist, is a favourite style, and ;proves very becoming to a good figure. Useful 'hats to accompany these costumes are in hairy cloth or felt, with the brims and crowns stitehed, or piped, so as to give quite j_i_ppearance 'to' the 'tat: The, -shapes ii.re'smalaCTB'ff crowns and brims curling upwards at the edge. A small compact turban shape is al sb favoured, trimmed solely with a Wing or plum'ae. White beaver hats are smart,and dainty looking.,

Th he ha oecaE at ' nat' ti'.

I c't. I°w. tip

' Many people seem to think that heliotrope in ;the more purple shades—true heliotrope colour, in fact—and grey will be moat fashionable during the autumn, and black or black and white will, of course, still be prevalent, as the Court is, un- happily, in deep mourning again. Frieze will still be popular, but fine, rather glossy-surfaced cloths will be most fashionable, and one of these in a rich rather dark heliotrope should suit well. So far as styles are concerned, corse:et skirts and short, loose sacques will be very fashionable. I fancy the reign of the bolero is nearly at at end, and of the coat* I have seen at present they are chiefly, the loose saeques, the new., bassuelees blouse-coat with pointed waist-belt, little coats with loose fronts and fitting backs, reaching to the hips, half and three-quarter-length gangues, and paletota.

Several shooting costumes are being madse with the skirt and bodice in one, princess fashion, fastening down one side in front to below the waist, with a double row of large buttons. Such frocks require to be very well cut and fitted, and have the seams piped with leather; leather pieces are stitched across the shoulders to prevent rub• bing. Lace vests and fancy cravats are entindy out of place with a sporting costume the whoko .iote of which should be a well-appointed severity. *The small chemisette showing at the throat shoald be of finely tucked linen or lawn, with a narrow turned-down hem-stitched collar and a neat cam- bric or silk bow in front.

The fashion for motor-car driving has become so great that the gentle art must decidedly be reckoned with when planning out a sporting outfit. One coat I saw is in bis-brown summer cloth, with coachman's cape collar of stitched taffetas in the same colour. A small breast-pocket on the left side proves useful. The hat is of stitched white taffetas, with soft pliant brim, simply trimmed with a large chou of red and white foulard, a white gauze veil enveloping most of the hat and the face, the ends brought round and tied at the side. A golfing dress had a plain, well-fitting skirt, with strappings round the hips and down the front, of cloth. The light, reversible cape of frieze one side and tartan the other, is slung from the shoulders by straps, and has a large, comfortable hood, which can be drawn up over the head in case of wind or wet.

Gauged effects are to be prominent this autumn, and for the between seasons are made hanging boleros, to give the short sack coat its newest name, very much gathered on the shoulders to form a yoke, and then covered with transparent vandyke lace collars. An Empire gown, especially when it hangs loosely from the shoulder line, must ho worn with an Empire petticoat, a garment that also hangs from the shoulders and is skirt and cache-corset in one. It is very commonly made of soft silk to form a foundation, but sometimes fine white lace insertioned lawn, fully trained of course, composes it. Your loving JEN:ing.

To BRIGHTEN' CARPETS.—Sometimes a Brussels carpet seems clean, but the colours have become dull and dingy. Put a teaspoonful of turpentine in half a gallon of warm water, dip a rag in it ancl wipe the carpet. Of course it should be thor. oughly swept first. The turpentine brightens tho colours wonderfully.

" "Who would give a vrretoliadTamateur work," r asked, "when there must be so many clever girls . who are professionals ?" . . • "I will give you work. All you can do. Don't ' look like that, please. 1, It's no ..pharity,' I assure ' you. I have lost my secretary and I need another. You can learn typing ina week; and in three or four menths, in leisure-hours, you will have made good progress with shorthand. As for the machine, I have two, and :ode tthem I'll—lend to you." ' "Thank you for stopping to • think in time and not saying give,' " I laughed. I "How good it is to hear that laugh," he said. "Now, are you glad you didn't go down under the dark waters 3" • "Oh, so glad!" impulsively. I held out my ' hands and he caught them both, gripping Wein.; so tightly that the grasp gave me pain. Then„suddenly, he let my hands go, with a changing expression that I wished -I had the chic to read. • "Mrs. Jennett shill find you just the right rooms," he went on, with a hint of sadness in his • voice. "Meanwhile, you shall be practising your new art, which will be all the easier for you if you know the piano, as of course you do. And in a few days, perhaps, you will be able to move. Then • you.'.ll.be soon earning three guineas a week, on which, if you are a very prudent young lady, you . can live quite pinfortably." "Dear me, can I really earn as pinch ps,that by typewriting?" I asked, opening my eyes. "It is more than I could have hoped for as a governess. Even with my music and languages, the 'people' at the agencies told me, before they grew disagreeable, that I could not ask at most more than forty pounds a year." ; `Oh, well, you see, people often take advantage of their governesses; whereas expert typewriters are pretty well paid," he informed me with a grave face—so grave that I never thought of doubting him. "Shall I come back to-morrow with the machine, and give you your first lesson?" At the end of a week I could write very well. Mrs. Jennett had found me rooms, whith were to be ready in a few days, but as they were still in occupation, I must stay where I was till they were ; free. I had grown to love the study and every book in it. 1 should be homesick when I went away. As for John Bourke, I would not let myself think what it would be when the days should go by without a glimpse of him. 1 had begun to live in the present, little dreaming of the precipice that my feet had all but reached.

MY NATIVE HILLS.

r'roM mountain tops descending to the plain, In peace 1 viewed my native fields again ; l'ht, whitened house upon the green old hill, Sunshine and shadow lingering o'er th3m still. ihe crystal stream which gathers as it goes, And rippling through tne peaceful valley flows: While on its bank i rich woods in beauty vie, Through which the rustling winds of autumn sigh. When oorml clouds break on the mountains breast, And floods of rain o'er all the valleys rest ; "O'er boulders huge the tumbling waters splash, And through deep rocks the roaring torrents dash. When days are short and autumn's soft winds blow, When nights are cold and all is white with snow 'vbenstars so brightly glitter in the sky, • Sublime in splendour are the mountains high. When spring returns how fresh the sunny air, ,Amid these scenes so lonely, sweet and fair; , " here brock and calder travel to the sea, ' Where I was born and would for ever be. , Compelled by fate in early rears to stray, 1,...And wander from my chilcnood's borne away ; "rt.-nigh life fot ever must the wish remain, To live beside my native hills again.

S. KENYON. Warrington, August, 1901.

IHE EXAMINER,

FRI i )AY, AVG, El

.an aces not niove anAncn in a 3111111071 years; ann. the ever-renewing hosts of life are flung against it, and perish before it, in vain. But youth, when it is tender of •lieart- and lofty 'f sou', does not believe this; it thinks such truths the embittered I exaggeration of cynics.

fri these times of anguish- Nerina, herself miserable, redoubled her devotions and attentions to her lady: a peaceh on a vine-leaf, a honeycomb on an old china platter, a trout from the river, a bunch of Centofoglie roses, a flask of Falerian— something or other she always found, and brought in, as an 'offering for holy days, and at night scarcely dared let herself sleep lest her mistress should be ailing and want her. Thom simple things were nothing in .themselves, of course, to the aching heart of the anxious mother; but to the affection of her homely servant, which sought for and found them, she, in all her - sorrow, was not insensible. The coming of the post was, as it is to sb many in days of suffering, the supreme event of the day. Nerina saw that the rare letters which did arrive brought tears more often than smiles to the worn face of the lady ;-.and.:when she took a letter•in her hand from the. postman at the house-door, she shook it, and.smelit-it, and stared at-it, and would have given her soul to have known what was inside it, so that if , it brought anxiety, she would have burnt it before it. could have reached Madame Tines , If she had known how .to read, she would not have had any scruples about opening an envelope tc see if the contents were messages of good or evil: and if she had found them the latter, would have thought it as right to have destroyed the missive as to stamp upon a scorpion. •With- strong and primitive natures fidelity does not hesitate at evil: if evil can spare ahy injury to the one beloved, it becomes ne longer an evil, but a duty, a virtue, a heroism. Nerina could not have reasoned about it, but it was thus that she felt. .To ,screen her lady from Calamity, there was no crime which would not. have seemed sanctified to her. One winter these uncanny atoms 'of written -paper, which bad such magical power (she saw) te wound and to delight seemed to cause continual anxiety to her mistress ; especially those which came from Rornanino. "He is vexing his soul again," thought Nerina. "0 Lord! why cannot he content himself with his brushes and sticks of Charcoal, and let the world mend its way itself, or roll out of purgatory into hell as it will." She had never heard of 11 Maretto of Brescia. But his was a kind of life she would have wished ' her young master to lead ; always in the same little city; always painting pictures of the sainte, growing grey in his native air, and leaving at *death his name as a sacred trust to his fellow- citizens—all his life a noble, calm, beloved figure, pacing the same 'stones froni the cradle to tho grave, and in his tomb beloved and honoured. ; in the spring which-followed that winter, the "Maladetta pri maven." of 1898, there was great • trouble in the country. There were riots in Many provinces, and hungry crowds pillaged and burnt, and the roar of cannon woke the echoes of many a street in many a town, and terror silenced the moans of a tortured people. In the little city of Maclaine Tina all was quiet. The echoes of the cannonades and the shrieks of the wounded did not reach this high crest of the rock, and the church belle rung as peacefully as ever, and the daffodils blossomed along the river banks, and the sun set and rose, and the moon- light shone on the red-grey moss-grown roofs; and only Madame Tina knew the terrible disquiet and desperation Which there were elsewhere. The letters told her—wicked, white owlets of woe, as they appeared to be to her woman. Romanino had crossetl from Venice to throw in his lot with the revolutionists in that terrible - Maggio Milanese, and had been fighting with the populace in the streets of Milan, and what had been his after-fate no one could tell. He had been seen and heard 'of in Lombardy, so much his mother only heard from others. In irresistible need of someone to share her torture of anxiety she told Nerina all she feared, one day, when the letter-carrier passed down the steep street and did not stop—the seventh time he had n6t stopped. She was anxious for all ; for the eldest in the Americas, for the second in Erithrea; but most of all for the youngest, the beautiful -ivy with his altruistic visions, hurled on the harsh reality of brutal facts, and probably flying for his life acros3 the Lombard Alps. . "Ser Romaninol 0 Ser Romanino " sobbed the servant, covering her head with her apron. She had him .fondled on her knee, , a child of four years old, like one of the golden-haired Putti on the church altar. But still her heart was wrathful and sore against him. He made his mother suffer. "What do all those other folks matter to him?* she thought, indignantly. "You should have kept him here, Madame," she said to her lady, "you should have kept him here, away from those mad, strange people." "By force?" answered his mother. "To see him die like a caged bird, beating his wings? Oh, no. Besides; believe me, he is right, he does right." Nerina, afraid she might utter hasty words, went away to her stove in the kitchen, and her tears fell fast into the hot charcoal and the palm leaf fan with Which she roused its sparks. They did not even knosi whether he were not dead in Milan or swept away into the crowded prison as so many others had been. The rest of the week went by ; seven more days, and still another seven, and there was no news. No one heard or knew anything except garbled information from public prints, and very little of that, nothing which anyone could be sure was true. News travels at all times very slowly ;still, to these isolated places, and in periods of insurrection, there is no information -at all which is act prepared and doctored by authority before it reaches the public. Again a third week passed, and Madame Tina heard nothing of her youngest son. She dared not make any inquiries lest she should do him harm; he might be dead or in prison. She could not eat or sleep. Her strength, never great, failed her entirely; and nothing that Nerina could do could give her hope or solace. She crept out to the old church and prayed there for hours-for her beloved boys. Never in her life had a lament escaped her, but now suffering broke down her patience and she Wailed aloud. ' "We bear them with pain, and rear them with hardship, and what is the use? We are like the poor COWS and sheep, who have all the woe of travail only to see their off-spring snatched away and done to death." "What can I do for her? " thought Nerina, in desperation. "I would cut off my right hand for her, and I can do nought." Madame Tina grew thinner and paler and niore shadow-like with every day which left her without news. She would not harken to her doctor, or take any tonic or cordial. "There is no cure for me," she said, "save to hear the voice of my youngest born." And perhaps that voice was for ever mute. "Hark ye, woman," said the old doctor of the town to Nerina where she W 133 beating linen in the river. "Whenever any letter comes to your lady you must bring it to me before she sees it, for if it give her 'bad news of the boy she will die on the stroke of it." •

"Bring'it to you? " said Nerina doubtfully. "Aye, aye," said the leech, "for if Romanino have beet), shot, as we think, it must be broken to her gently, very gently ; her heart is as weak as a thread of gossamer." The leech, the only one in the little town, was mold friend of the family, a grey-haired, brown faced, long, lean man, wrapped lip, no matter what weather it was, in a large black cloak,with slouch hat over his eyes. He was a familiar figure on the bastions and in the narrow streets, and kept a pharmacy where jars and vases and big- bellied bottles of old ina3olica were in company with bundles of dried simples and herbs and charms of the middle ages. He was greatly esteemed and respected. "How shall I know what letter comes from him?" she asked. "All letters look alike to me." "Then bring me all." said Ser Lillo, as he was called by his sick folks. "'Tis a matter of life and death to Madama Tina. If she have a shock she will die of it." And he argued the question so earnestly, so frequently, and with such persistence that she felt as if her mistress's life WAS in her hands. It was the letters, the cruel letters, which had been like a death-potion to her lady. "I have often a mind to fling them on the char- coal," she said. "They stab her to the heart liko knives." "No, no, that would be wrong," said the doctor. "They are not yours to destroy. Bring them to me. I will judge if they be such as Will .do her no harm. Of course, whatever news they bring she must know it some time, but softly, gently, by slow .degree." Was it right to do this? Nerina was perplexed. A FAITHFUL SERVANT.

Dv OUIDA,

Author :,of "Under Two Flags," "Puck," "Two Little Wooden Shoes," "A Dog of Flanders," "Moths," "The Massarenes," &c.

Nerina Taccari was a woman of fortr•five years of age; she was a brown; ,comely, stout person, finely-built and strongly-made, with a smile like sunshine, and teeth as white as a dog's, and brown eyes which, Were apt to have storms in them at times when the stupidity or viciousness of other folk t provoked her: She, had been, both in a'little hamlet on the Sabine hills; high up on a mountain spur, where torrents ran, and snows often gathered; and, far down below, one of the greatest and fairest scenes of earth was outspread, as in frescoes by old masters a volume lies open on the knee's of Jehovah. She had been married at fourteen years of age to a herdsman of the Campagna, and before she was twenty had known most of the trials of life; hunger, ill-treatment, and the bearing and loss of children, the fatigue- of 'ill-fed toil, and the injustice of a spouse who expected her to make bread with stones. When she approached her thirtieth year her husband, after what seemed to her a lifetime of woe, was killed by one of his bulls ; the animal, goaded into just rage by his cruelty, felled and tossed him, and the whole herd passed triumphantly over his ,body, whiclu was trampled into a mere mass of bleeding grass- stained pulp. Nerina herself ,could not have recognised it when it was brought on a hurdle home to her hut, a conical pile of red tufa, Stones and turf, built _under the shadow.of the Castel Giubileo. Left withdut a support and ordered :by the owners of the herd to leave the tufa cabin, in which she and her spouse had lived, slue rent back' to her own people in the, hills, and thence into service with a family she knew, to get away from the many miserable memories, and hunger and toil, which had been alone her portion on the Agro Romano. She was mountain-born, and the heat and drought of the plains were hateful to her. The little city to which she went was in a topmost spur of her own Sabine Apennines—a small grey ancient place, with gigantic, walls and marble ruins and tenth-century houses, clustered round a Longobardo church. ,It still retained its thirteenth century ramparts, and a deep, though narrow stream foamed beneath its bastions, rushing -down over rocks and through gorges to tumble into ,the Licenza, which 'in ,its turn fed the Arno ere Arno fell into the Tiber. Here shekpent sixteen peaceful years ; working hard, but 'living as one of the family and attaching herself to them with the affection of a dog. They were the people who had ever been good to her. Her mistress, Caterina Lorenzetti, always called Madama Tina by her neighbours and servants, Was a widow with three young sons, who had means enough to live with a certain ease, though frugally and simply, in a small venerable house which looked from the ramparts on the valley of the Arno far below, and had withstood many a rough time of siege and assault in the wars between the Popes and the, Lords of Tivoli and Palestrina, of Subiaco and Olivano, and of all the walled villages and grim strongholds which then frowned in the face of the setting sun. Nerina loved the merry, good-natured, handsome lads, but she adored their mother, who had never said a harsh word to her ; although her clumsiness and ignorance and __violence in the first years had sorely tried the patience of Aladama Tina, who had been reared in a convent, and possessed by both nature and habit, and strong instincts of order, tranquillity, and (aim "If I turn her out," the good lady replied to the counsels of her neighbours in those early days, "I shall have a crime on my conscience, for she knows nothing and is of a violent temper; in desperation she might do something rash ; her heart is good, and I hope to guide her aright, so that she, can take her own way safely as time goes on when I shall be no more."

........._ ..., .....„. , Her patience did not fail her in her task, and" Nerina became a grateful and capable servant; never iriry peaceable. quiet or accomplished, but unremitting in effort, devoted in sickness, useful in all homely, hardy ways, and filled with a passion of loyalty and love for "Madame Tina and the three bright boys who had all the old seraphic beauty of Italy in their handsome faces, and who, if they sometimes plagued her, loved her warmly in return. " Every drop of water which was used in the house she brought up in bronze pails from the well on the rampart; she went for the milk and did all the marketing; she made the bread .and cooked the polenta ; she scrubbed and swept and scoured all the coppers, and all the stairs and floors; she went to Mass at five o'clock in the morning in all weathers; she washed all the linen in the river which ran beneath the city wall; and in her few leisure hours spun and sewed for the family and herself. It was a hard life, but she was content in it, and even enjoyed every hour in it with,the vigour of a sane and robust temperament, a healthy constitution, and a grateful soul. It was so much to her to sleep soundly without ' fear of a brute kicking her off the bed of leaves on to the ground; and to hear the placid voice of her mistress, and the gay songs of the children, instead of the curses of the herdsmen and the lewd jokes of the shepherds in drink. She would have asked nothing more of the saints than to let this life go on for ever. "Only let me be worthier," she said in her prayers every time that she knelt down in the little dusky side aisle where she went for confession in the Longobardo church. In this town, perched so high, at a greater altitude than even the rocks of the Sagro Spepo, the winters are exceedingly cold, and the summers cruelly hot; in midsummer the stones burn the feet which tread them, and in winter the icicles hang from the fountains and water spouts, and the winds moan round the battlemented walls, as the mercenaries (A Borgia and Farnese, .Orsini and Barberini, were wont to ride beneath them in days of seige when all the country beneath was burning in the internecine wars of the pontiffs and princes. But Nerina, was a strong mountain-born woman who did not heed such small things as heat and cold, and she came and went, up and down the steep streets, like a gust of wind herself. Several offers of marriage were made her by men of the town, by men of the hills above, and men of the plains below, but she would have none of them. "Never more a master for me," she said to her mistress. "I am out of the net of the fowler; never more will I get in it; not I." And this plain, peaceful tenour of her days might have gone on until death should have taken her,L but for the restlessness of others which caught her in its eddy as a swirling stream whirls away with it a bit of mops, a dead leaf, a snapped twig. The beautiful tall boys grew up, and went away, one by one; the eldest, who was a priest, was sent by his church to Brazil ; the second, perforce, became a soldier, and was in Africa; the third went to study painting in Venice. The young artist came back at intervals, but rarely ; the two others had not returned, and the old grey house was silent and sad; the two women wept together, and all differences of rank and education were forgotten in a common sorrow. In a degree, however, the tenacious fiery soul of Nerina rejoiced. "Mada Ma. Tina has no one now but me," she thought • and then her better self smote her for such selfishness ;-how could she ever make up to the mother for the absence of the three sons ?"I'in only a poor, stupid servant," she said to herself with contrition. The one who hal gone to study art was the youngest, by name Romanino, a lad of twenty, with a face like the Pitti Ganymede's, and a tem- perament tender, impulsive, heroic, full of dreams. is mother had heavy anxieties about him which the could not con Ede to the unlearned brain of her woman. Yet Nerina vaguely understood the dreams which had sent Romanino, their darling, into danger. "Ser Romanino wants to make the world anew," she said; "and it's too bad, and too old, and too cruel ; he will only break his heatl on it, as the poor mules break theirs climbing up over the stones of these steep streets." _ - She understood,only_s little, vaguely; but some- thing she understood: Romanino had talked to her so much, leaning his curly head against tier knees, and indifferent tie the capacity of his auditor in the ardour of his own eloquence. "He wants to have nobody hungry, or cold, or shoeless any more," she said to her mistress. "Dear, sweet soul! lle might as well Wish the stones of the ramparts to turn into wheaten loaves. There are the gorged pigs on one side of the world and the famished' dogs en the other; and there always have been, and there always will be. Tell him so, lkladama."- His mother did tell him so; but the youth in . Rotuanino believed in its power to move mountains, as' generous, noble, and holy youth ever haa done, Ind ever will do, to the end of time. The moun-

At midnight she and Madame Tina were waiting in the little damp garden behind the house, which opened by a postern door on the bastion. As the stroke of midnight sounded from the church clock a young voice whispered, very low: "'Tis I—Nina. Are you there?" Nerina wrenched the heavy door open; the mother pressed forward and "fell into her son's arms. At the same mcment three Communal guards E eized him, leaping from the shelter of the laurel thicket on the rampart. They dragged him out from his mother's arms and, struck her servant to the ground. Nerina gathered herself up, blind, sick, reeling. "Beasts! How did you know? " The guards laughed brutally, and dragged Ilornanino away in the gloom of the faint oil-light, their pistols held to his temples, their hand-cuffs on his wrists. 31adama Tina had dropped, to the ground in syncope. She was carried into her -house and into her chamber. She died in an hour's time. The doctor stood by her bed, having Spent all his science in vain. Be seemed kind, pitying, wise. "My poor soul, you shotifd go to your room and rest after such a shock," he said to Nerina, where she sat mute by the corpse. She had never spoken a word since the seizure of Romanino. It was now late in the forenoon. She stared at him continually with her dry, fierce eyes, and' the gaze made him uneasy and troubled. "I can do no more good here," lie said with regret, a little later, and rose and left the chamber ofdeath. The parish priest accompanied him. All the last services of life were rendered that • night by the servant to her mistress. No other hand but her servant's touched the body of Madame, Tina. All 'the thirty hours which followed Nerina watched by the bed, watched by the bier, 'seemed 'dumb, seemed -deaf: seemed blind. When all was over and she alone had been the mourner at tlie tomb, her head covered and her face muffled in a black, woollen shawl, she slunk like a lost 'wounded animal under the shadow of the church, where her lady had been left for ever, raid in her leaden coffin beside the dust of her forefathers. Distinct, in the bewildered torment of her thoughts one supreme idea stood out as written in fire. How had they known? Who had told them of Romanino's return? , As she went slowly across the few score of yards 1 which separated the church from the house which had sheltered her through so many years, she saw ,a loan, black, tall form passing across the road- way,. It was the figure of the apothecary. A sudden light like that 'of an electric flash thrOugh a murky night pierced the stupor of her dull brain. There was the traitor—there! With a bound she was upon him, her hands clutched his shoulders, her tearless,. hollow eyes blazed into his. "Yon sold their 'secret!" Startled and thrown off his guard by the sudden charge he, stammered denial, grew livid, shook in all his limbs. "You sold their secret!" "No, no," he muttered. "No, no! What I did I did from duty. The law must be obeyed." "You sold their secret!" It was all clear to her as though a voice from 'Heaven or from hell spoke in her ear. He had laid his trap for her to learn the truth for the law- makers, and had been paid by the law like Judas. He had made her betray the dead. He had made her betray the living. "You sold their secret!" she said for the fourth time, and she read his guilt upon his face Where 'the yellow flicker of his lamp before the shrine fell on it. "Ah, wretch! Ah, bealt! Ah, Judas!" she sereamed in his ear, and with all her might she clutched him in her strong arms and swept him off his feet as though he were a broken, rootless tree. WITH the close of the

The Reckoning session the country will

want to know -what are its results. If we attempt to strike a balance, the effort involves us in issues that are appalling in their vastness, and their magnitude is the meas- ure of the country's loss, for it must be admitted there is a heavy deficit balance against profit and loss account. With the National Debt increased by two hundred millions and another hundred in prospect, with taxation at sixty millions a year, with the Civil List increased to nearly half a mil- lion, and the same lavish increase in all the depart- ments of the public service, what have we to show in the way of assets? A. couple of laige colonies added to the British Empire, which for some years to come will be in a state of chronic bankruptcy, lost prestige in China, jealousy and heartburning against this country covered by a thin veil of amity on the part of the other great Powers of Europe. A war unended and unending, still going on like a running sore sapping the vitality of the Empire, and which may still be described in the pathetic words used in the King's Speech when he opened Parliament as "not entirely terminated." A large army scheme, eating its head off, a large expenditure on the navy and on . military and naval works, exceeding anything which this country has ever known in the past. Increased taxation and decreasing trade, with the prospect of falling wages and small returns in all departments of industry. This is the outcome of that administration which is supposed to be the strong point of the present Government. True, we have a consolidated Commonwealth in Australia, and we have spent a considerable amount of money in a royal tour to celebrate the occasion. But it is still to be made clear how the advantages which we generally confer upon our Colonies are to be reciprocated, or how the United Kingdom is to be relieved of the very unfair pro- portion which it at present bears of Imperial government and Imperial defence. There is not ' much in all this to gratify or cheer the heart of the most ardent optimist. Do we turn to home affairs—that region which Blenheim Unionists are called upon to enter with courage and persistence —what do we find? Out of eight proposed measures, which may be taken to be in the opinion of the Cecil Cabinet of first-class importance, for they were promised in the KIN-G's Speech, two have been passed into law. The Drunkard's Dill was and " is not " ; the promised legislation en a Final Court of Appeal has not come into being, neither has that on Irish land purchase—though a good measure was rejected by the Government—neither has that respecting lunatics, nor London water supply, nor literary copyright. The Factories and Workshops Bill is the chief asset the Government can claim in home legislation; and good and use- ful so far as it goes, it carries with it the marks of humiliation and defeat. The Education Bill is the other asset. But it is not the Bill promised in the speech from the throne. It is another and a feebler, though not a less mischievous measure than the one that is still to con*, of which it is but the echo in

advance. A measure the keynote of which is the destruction of the democratic principle in that most democratic of all popular necessities,national education. The Government, having slipped the habital drunkard, took hold of the child messenger and became his guardian angel. But it was a silly, pretentious guardianship that reduced his age from sixteen to fourteen, allowed him to run all the risks of the tap-room if only the bottle was corked, or if he were in the employ of the tapster. Such a sham assumption of interest in temperance reform may well have drawn the wail from the lips of Lord SritNcEn"which he uttered on Friday, that the Government might have turned their large majority to much better account ja checking the crying evil of the age, intemperance But one does not gather grapes of thorns nor figs of thistles, and it is folly to look] for temperance. legislation from a Tory Government. There is one thing, though, that may be looked for. It is endowment—endowment of the farmer, che land" lord and the priest. That, is where the Doles Bill comes in ; it is a fresh instance of the ingenious methods by which the Cecil Cabinet have managed to convey the money of the people to the pockets of their friends. It was not in the King's speech. Probably its authors were ashamed of it. At all events, it practically makes the complement of Government legislation for the session. This is the full tale of bricks. We can scarcely hope the country will be satisfied with the reckoning. The only:question is how:long the electors will stand it.

INTERNATIONAL *whit - International Arbitration, ration is a subject of perennial interest, an (I although by many it is scoffed at asamong the iiv. practicabilities of day-dreamers, it is significant that its ultimate triumph is considered hopeful to the extent of certainty by a body of men the least ' given to weak sentiment or extravagant ideals The International Law Association, which is now having its twentieth conference at Glasgow, is s body of lawyers, and, as the Lord PROVOW re- marked with probable truth, a body containing the best and highest trained intellects in the world. It is certainly a strong point iu favour of any decision at which they may arrive that these gentlemen, in taking up the study of international law, and seeking thereby to find ways and means of removing occasions of controversy and strife between nations, caa have no hope of immediate personal reward, no post or office to gain from sucli a philosophic study. Perhaps if, as the result Of their researches, they can confer upon the nations of the earth the boon of perpetual surcease frore the barbarities of physical warfare, they will be forgiven much in civil life on account of which the ordinary lay mind holds a grudge against them. 15 is, perhaps, well to bear in mind that there is much in the emperience and consequent point of view of the lawyer which specially qualifies him to pronounce an opinion on the probability 0E arbitration becoming a practical solution of inter- national difficulties. They have daily evidence in the lawsuits they are conducting of strong rancor' ous feeling on the part of litigants being repress' ed, and made subject to the ruling of the 101 courts; they can look back as a matter of history to the time when such disputes were settl the individuals themselves by resort to violence'ed y which frequently resulted in interminable f amilf feuds rather than any kind of settlement. The/ see that under the improving influences of b tion a litigant comes to the law courts, has '" fling, and as a rule, retires thereafter, either to sir joy the costly satisfaction of victory or to el.° the cud of bitter reflection. This is at least bette.r for the community, and may be even a boon 1, disgnise to the individual. The lawyers argue Witu. some ground in reason that such a resolution in civil life affords a sufficient precedent for an equalll improved condition in our international relatio°6l' andlthe International Law Association is engaged in searching for the best metliod which will usher in that day when, in the words of AflamitAot "Law will be sovereign of the world." The advair tage of such a conference as the association hes brought together is that it affords an opportunitY for the free expression of opinion from all the nationalities interested in this great question. 15 is consoling to hear an American, for instance, re' gretting the failure of the British-American Arbi- tration Treaty, which had well-nigh brought a hal° of real glory to Lord SALISBURY, and to hear it said that America might' now be disposed to accept gladly a repetition of the offer she so foolishly re- jected. It is also • gratifying to find a French doctor speaking practically with the unanimous poles of the most enlightened and responsible por tion of his countrymen when he supported wits cordiality the proposal for such a treaty betwees France and Great Britain. There are difficulties of course. But no great question is without thea3 ; and it is to the solution of these that the associe- tion in its conference is addressing itself.

is separated from the lower by a canvas 'partition placed deck-wise across the cavity, so that the upper portion forms the balloon proper, and will contain pure hydrogen gas, which in lifting power is much superior to ordinary coal gas, and therefore gives greater force in less bulk. The lower cavity is divided into three c ompart- tcents, those fore and aft being retained purely as it chambers, which may be filled with gas if desired. The central compartment will contain, the whole accommodation and working apparatus of the ship, which will serve the double purpose of ballast, effectually preventing it from turning turtle," and that of keeping the whole gearing within easy reach of the .°Perator. This central chamber will afford • accommodation for cabin, state-room, larder, engine-house and fuel-bunker. It will have a door on either side, and a porthole fore and aft, which Nina serve for look-out purposes. The motive Power will be supplied by an oil-motor, which will drive a pair of four-blade propellers similar to the .our-bladed screw of a great steamer. These pro- pellers, however, project from the sides of the ulachine, and they form the main feature of Mr. )31%11AN-a/c's invention, They are constructed of light material, each blade measuring five feet by three, and being grooved diagonally, so as to have an appearance similar to that presented by corru- 'gated iron. It is claimed for this process that the blade takes a better grip of tke air, and therefore

_ secures a more effective thrust in its revolution. he angles of the propellers may be varied so as to assist in the steering and M the raising and lowering of the ship, to which they materially con- tribute. The constructors of the ship are as san- 'guine as the inventor that it offers greater proba- bilities of success than any invention that as yet aP,eared ; and as it is expected to be in readiness the Pith proX. the British public will look for- rd to the coming trials with greater interest lan ever.

The saying that there are two things no French- Plan can escape, death and the Legion of Honour, 148,1farnortal. It is also a not undeserved sneer at multiplication of "hononrs" which has been °I,;(10f the most peculiar features of the century ;Zs Past. In England honours have increased, as rapidly as in any country. Public ser- `c,e o• is no doubt deserving of honour, and it car- 'airily cannot be said that the powers who dispense the decorations have been miserly in the matter Of rewards for the efficient discharge of public duties. An ingenious correspondent now desires to institute yet another source of such reward ; this time for the special recognition of the county 'lnd borough councillor, the guardian of the poor, officials of the bodies to which these people belong. The President of the Local Government -Oar a has promised to consider the matter, and 110 doubt he will do so from all possible points of view. It is quite true that the ordinary I)lunicipal legistator generally goes unrewarded Tor services to his town, unless by some happy Chance he is mayor during a Royal Visit; but we 4re not at all sure that for purely local services , purely local recognition of the confidence and honour of his fellow-townsmen is not the most ade- quate and suitable recognition. The multiplica-1 t.ion of honours and rewards tenth to bring them 111 to disrepute, and an honour which is not valued 1011 y had t etter never be conferred. Tiacn, again, it has hitherto been the glory of our municipal 'flatitutions that the services rendered by their embers were services rendered for the good of I,"ge community, and freely without thought or VPe of reward. It is only too probable that with ;Pecial "Order of Public Service " the grateful 4ifice of municipal service would be lowered in the 4blic estimation, and the hope of reward would the minds at least of the people occupy an on- Pleasantly prominent position in the calculations ef local administrators.

--- It is an old cry that the English farmer does not cater to the extent that is possible for the batter and cheese markets of the large towns. The fact is obvious enough, and its repetition can do no harm . It does not seem, however, to be always realised by the farmer's critics that this neglect may is be altogether a waste of opportunity. He has,, it s true, let the trade slip out of his hands; nut that he makes no sustained effort to recover i it s due to the fact that his energies and capital are devoted to meeting the vastly increased de- n3and for milk from the large towns. In most eases, where milk finds a ready sale, it is better, 11:0In a business point of view, to dispose of it . that way, and modern railway facilities .tnake it passible to do so at a re- , rnunerative rate for the great majority of dairy farmers. For those otherwise situated, Utter-making offers the greatest chances of profit, If Only farmers could be induced to take the pains .J 0 make it of the highest quality, and to ,make it W :Is far as possible uniform in character. e have heard recently of sojourners in some of the most famous dairying counties sending up to London for their butter, owing to the unreliable quality of the article to be procured in the npighbourhood ; and though some margin must be allowed for zaggeration, there is undoubtedly sufficient, M truth these statements for them to make an uncom- fortable impression. The farmer should have no difficulty in producing butter vastly superior to that of the factory make, whereas, on account of its uniform quality, the town consumer begins actually to prefer the latter.

The holidays were responsible for the postpone- ment of the meeting of the Warrington Town Coun- cil until Tuesday evening, last week, and the holi- days, we suppose,must also be held responsible for the diminished attendance. The absentees included more than half of the Aldermen, who are supposed tO lend a certain amount of experience and dignity to the proceedings. To them the near approach (If November does not prove the same incentive for brethren in municipal work as to those less favoured °rethren who may be c4lled upon at an earlf date 'to give an account of their stewardship to their ]enstituents. Notwithstanding the smaller atten- 'ance the flow of oratory was fully niaintained,and lor nearly two hours the Council discussed various 141)ics of more or less importance.

„ Sankey Brook was again the subject of dis- `,4, saion. No local subject has been so frequently ttscussed and yet so barren in its results. It at- most seems as though this continual talkee-talkee served as a stimulus to the offenders for the filth 'Qot into the brook increases in volume year by I,!ar and the stench increases proportionately. „lah have long since ceased to live in the brook and wonder is that the birds of the air do not shun this pestiferous locality. Formerly it was the Practice of local authorities to deny any liability tOr the pollution, but this much has been gained teeently, that the offenders are now willing c' sit upon the penitent stool and mak e full confession of their wrong- doing. 14111 is a healthy sign and may ultimately Zad to the stoppage of the nuisance. The War- ton Corporation has led the way with a pro— Posal to ” treat the sewage from this Borough on !,:.e„elving an assurance that all otiser authorities t_ku treat the sewage from their respective (tie- ets," At a meeting of the Warrington Rural Zlatrict Council on Wednesday a similar course (3" adopted. Armed with these assurances the o,lltity Council may be able to induce all the con- a'zbutory authorities to the nuisance to mutually .enles to treat their sewage. It must not be for- as the Mayor pointed out, that the eree- coll of the new asylum at Winwick must lead the thillantY authorities to take a deeper interest in ksb, matter, for as the controlling authority of the se,„",!tlrmi Board the County Council will have to 40-I'o it that the crude sewage of the asylum is turned into the Brook, or it will be of little thin asking local authorities to stop doing some- Which they themselves are guilty of. 'What may be termed the after effects of the.

construction of the Manchester Ship Canal figured largely in the agenda of ths Council meeting. ' They form a pretty collection and are matters which call for prompt action and earnest protest. I The first of these matters which received the attention of the Council was the recommendation /of the Health Committee that the Corporation should oppose in the House of Lords the Manches- ter Port Sanitary Authority Bill. By this Bill it is sought to make Warrington contribute towards the expenses of the Port Sanitary Authority which has been brought into existence by the construction of the Manchester Ship Canal. When the Bill came before the House of Commons Committee the Warrington Corporation opposed it and pointed out its inequity, by showing that Warrington does not derive the slight- est benefit from the canal. The Corporation did not succeed in their object to be excluded from the Order and we are afraid tharea better fate does not await them in the House of Lords. At any rate the Corporation have decided to set forth the justice of their case once again in the hope of obtaining a more favourable decision. The second matter affecting the Ship Canal Com- pany which called for consideration was that of the inadequate dredging of the river. The Rivers Committee, acting on a complaint received from Messrs Monks,H all and Company, have instructed the Borough Surveyor to inform the Canal Com- pany that the plant employed in the dredging is altogether insufficient, and requiring the Company to put on additional dredgers. This is not the first complaint of the kind wnich has been addressed to the Ship Canal Company and it will be foe duty of the Corporation to take such steps as will preserve for us a navigable river.

The third subject relating to the Manchester Ship Canal Company called forth the righteous in- dignation of the Mayor. The Company has been graciously pleased to request the Warrington and Northwich Light Railway Company to pay them an imposition of £7OO per annum for the right to use the highways over the swing bridges at Stock- ton Heath and Knutsford-road, and the Light Railway Company has been so agreeable as to con- sent to such a payment. The Light Railway Corn- pany'slacquiescence is easily explained. By their agreement with the Corporation this little annual impost would fall upon the Corporation when they took over the proposed loop line of the light railway from Latchford to Stockton Heath. It was an audacious move on the part of the Manchester Ship C. "ras-1 Company to ask such a sum for the privilege of using the King's highway—a privilege which was in existence long before the Manchester Ship Canal was even I dreamt of The Mayor characterised the proposal' as being "as monstrous as it was idiotic,' but , even this strong language does not adequately describe the situation. There is some consolation in knowing that the impost will not be paid. The Light Railway Company have now been called upon to withdraw the loop line from the order' and this will pave the way for the Warrington Cor- poration to seek Parliamentary powers to enter these districts with tramways. •

A .more striking example of the difference of opinion between two medical men on the origin of a nuisance could hardly be afforded than that which we published last week in connection with what is now familiarly known as the Thelwall nuisance. It will be remembered that the Runcorn Rural District Council decided to call in an expert to in- vestigate the origin of an alleged nuisance arising from the Grappenhall Tannery and the brook which divides the townships of Grappenhall and Thelwall. Dr. Penny was the expert decided upon and his report has at length been published together with Dr Adams's observations upon it. Briefly stated, Dr Penny contends that the efflu- ent from Messrs Long's tanyard gives off at times, an offensive effluvium nuisance injurious to health. Dr Adams is of an entirely different opinion. He recognises that there is a nuisance at the junction where the various effluents meet, but he flatly denies that the tannery effluent is the source of the disease and deaths that have occured in Thelwall. He even goes farther and states that specific disease, such as that which has occurred in Thelwall, can- not be set up by effluvia, but must be generated by the bacillus of the disease itself. In the face of such a conflict of medical opinion we are not surprised that the Rural District Councillors were somewhat perturbed as to what course to take. In deciding to instruct the Sur- veyor to prepare a scheme in accordance with the Medical Officer's recommendations. which curiously anoughlagree generally with those of Dr. Penny,the Council adopted the right course. With the payment of Dr. Penny's little bill of 6:5 guineas, and the carrying out of the recommenda- tions, we hope this unpleasant episode will be brought to a close.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

When her Majesty the Queen lets it be publicly known that she hopes all ladies who are to be pre- sent at the Coronation " will employ for their dresses as much as possible material made by British manufacturers, embroidered by British workwomen," her wish ought to be equivalent to a command. British, of course, embraces Irish, and there can be no doubt as to the kindly teeling expressed in such a wish. The sums of money that will be expended by the great ladies who are expec- ted to attend the Coronation will be very large, and it will be an advantage to British trade that it b hall be spent in this country. There is no question here of the encouragement of inferior work for patriotic reasons, as the products of British factories and the work of skilled British women will bear favourable comparison with that to be obtained elsewhere. So that it is a perfectly legitimate occasion for sup- porting home industries, and the strintest political economist would be puzzled in his efforts to find anything to urge against it.

Gas compinieS and gas managers long since got over the scare created when electric lighting first began to get into vogue, and now some of them in our large towns and cities are said to be at their wit's end to know bow they are to meet the demand, among other domestic contrivances,for gas cookers, there are so many applicants. Cooking by gas was in fashion five and twenty or thirty years ago, and possibly more,but it is only of late that these stoves have come into very general use in the provinces. Manufacturers have introduced marked improve- ments, public demonstrations of their value have familiarised the public with them, but, more than all, the hiring system has led to that sudden popu- larisation which has almost outstripped the means for meeting the demand. The very extensive use of gas for beating as well as cooking, the adoption of incandescent mantles for lighting, and the wide application of gas power, show that the gas com- panies have still a very large field to occupy.

--- What do English tenant farmers think of this? The policy first urged by Mr. John Bright of "con- verting" Irish tenant farmers is making progress so rapidly that within the last twenty years. since the Act of 1881 was passed, the number of advances of sums of money to tenants is stated to have been 55,307, while the amount advanced totals up to £18,992,891 sterling. These are large figures, but what is more remarkable still is, that the arrears at the date of the report July 12) under the Act of 1885 were £2,421; and on November let last, under the Act of 1891, £Bl6 only was due. The report proves, at any rate, that the fears entertained by some of wholesale non-payment of the purchaae- money, were unfounded.

The perfection to which motor-cars have been ' brought is shown by their turning up in all sorts of unexpected places, and safely overcoming road difficulties of more than the ordinary kind. The other day an Irish telegram announced that an automobile 'party had astonished the natives by safely driving through the Gap of Dunloe,a journey familar :to tourists in the neighbourhood of Kul- ' larney. The motors came to no berm. From Geneva it is reported that a gentleman and his wife invaded the Alps and ascended the Great St. Bernard; and the Grand Duke Mikhaylovich has had a mountain tour in the Caucasus in a ten-horse motor-car. One of the war telegrams this week recounts that General French has permitted the use of railway bicycles and motor cycles, which attain a speed of thirty to forty miles an hour, the cycles apparently being in some way not revealed

===== 0002601_19010823_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER FRIDAY AUGUST 23 1901.

Ifitted to the railway lines. This at once suggests that if the railway mortor-cars are suitable for the carriage of troops they should be suitable also for , the conveyance of paesengers, and that the public should soon see these cars in operation on some of the branch lines of the English provinces. While the long distance fast trains have attained a high degree of excellence, the slow stopping trains in the provinces by which it is a misery to travel, are often found to be just the same now as they were twenty or thirty years ago. At any rate, they have not attained a speed equal to that of General French's railway motor-car, and their supersession by some such new method of traction would be one of the lest things that could happen in some of our rural districts.

Three months have now expired since the new dietary system at the Union Workhouses came into operation, and it is both interestinp and instructive to find Boards of Guardians reporting that. during the three months a material saving has been effEcted. Because there never had been in recent years any matter connected with the administra- tion of the poor law that gave rise to more deter- mined opposition, amounting in some cases to downright mutiny, than these new regulations, which were defended by the Local Government Department as framed in the interests of workhouse inmates, as well as with the idea of preventing waste of food. If in other workhouses besides those that have already reported, the experience is simi- lar, those who resented the order from headquarters may feel disposed to regret the bitterly antagonis- tic tone they recently adopted, while others are justified by their experience. If on the other hand there are Boards that believe there has been no saving it will be as well that they should say so, and that efforts should be made to account for the different exparience of the various authorities.

1 Municipal purity is fir-reaching, as many public bodies are beginning to discover. But there are other municipal author Wes, not above suspicion by any means, which do not appear to have yet realised the meaning of the phrase. The ruling , that interest in a gas company, or undertaking; ' precludes a town-councillor from legally voting on an electric lighting scheme in his borough may appear extreme, i but it is supported by high legal authorities. Consequently, at a special meeting of the Barnstaple Town Council this week to consider a motion hostile to an electric lighting scheme resolved upon in June lagt, the Mayor announced that under the Municipal Corporations Act no one pecuniarily interested in the local gas company could vote on the project,or even participate in the discussion. So many members appeared to b 3 interested in the gas company, that in the words of the reporter, this had the effect of " knocking the bottom out of the whole affair." The people of Barnstaple generally were in favour of the electric lighting scheme, which, it is said, will " now go ahead.' Other authorities either less informed or less squeamish, might with advantage to the com- munity make a note of this.

It may appear too good to be true, as the -saying goes, that some women are at present taking up the question in earnest which relates to " trailing dresses" and their effects upon the spread of con- sumption, thus defyieg Queen Fashion, and deferr- ing to the opinions of the medicos who attended the recent Medical Conference, and the congress on Tuberculosis. It is hoped, however, in the interests and for the well-being of the public, that the fashion itself will change, .and, then there will be still more solid reason for hope and confidence in this direction. Agitations for the abolition of ladies trains and trailing dresses have been attempted before, but died in their infancy. It says much for the medicos, however, that they have left so favourable and sensible an impression upon the minds of the gentler sex as to cause them to make a sacrifice even in their devotion to the goddess fashion, in the interests of the humanity of which they form a part. There are, of course, many other questions which may arise in this cion- nection,such,for instance, as the habit of expectora- tion on the footwaiks, the cleanliness of our streets and thoroughfares and so forth, but while awaiting the sweet will "of the local authorities to look to these matters, it should not be forgotten or overlooked that the individual can do much to make tuberculosis less common by personal compli- ance with the laws of health, and more strict at- tention to the requirements of this particular condition of things. Every one will, of course, wish the women's movement a great success.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FATAL RESULT OF THE EXPLOSION AT A WIDNES

WORKS.

On Friday afternoon one of the men injured last week at a Widnes works, named John C Singleton, 46, Kent-street, Widnes, died at the Accident Hospital at 3.30 in the afternoon. One or two of the injured men are also said to be in a critical condition.—On Monday afternoon an in- quest was opened at the Accident Hospital but was adjourned after formal evidence had been taken.

REINCORN BOATING CLUB REGATTA.

The unfinished races in connection with the above were completed on Saturday last, in very wet weather, and before a very meagre attendance.— The Club Championship (quarter-mile skiff) resulted as follows: Semi-final, W A Malinger beat Paohn- son, and J Savage beat H G Johnson; final, W A Malinger beat J Savage easily and secured the cup having won it for three years in succession.—The half-mile double pair scull race with cox, prizes three salad bowls, was won by Messrs J Savage, H Jones and T Green (cox), after an exciting race by a length. UNITED METHODIST FREE CHURCH, RUNCORN.

On Sunday the Rev. Walter Gay, lately of Prudhoe, Northumberland, commenced his ministry at this place of worship in succession to toe Rev. Samuel Wright,who has retired on superannuation. The rev. gentleman occupied the pulpit at both ser- vices, and appealed to the congregation for their undivided support in the work he had come to undertake. He pointed out that a vigorous church life depended upon both pastor and people realizing their respective duties and responsibilities, and en- deavouring to put into practice the teaching of Christ in their everyday life. BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH, RUNCORN. The annual choir sermons were preached at the above church on Sunday by Mr It El Posnett. The choir were en evidence with some special music in the form of anthems, solos,and quartettes from the works of Mendelssohn and other famous composers, and their efforts were characterised by a pr,!cision only acquired by careful rehearsal and training. A duet was rendered by Messrs T Worrall and W Shaw; a quartette by Misses Clare and Findlovv and Mer,srs Worrell and Shaw; and a trio by Masters J J Lunt, B Kant, and H Bate. These efforts were greatly appreciated, as were also those of Mr Posn'stt in the pulpit. Toe collections were satis- factory.

HALT ON.

HALTON PAROCHIAL COM NIITTEE MEETI T Wright, rural district Councillor, presided at the above meeting on Tuesday night,when there were also present: —Messrs J LookPr, J S Jaacock, W Moores, A Povey, George F Ashton (clerk to the Runcorn Rural District Council), and James Farrington (inspector).—A letter from Mr Evans, agent to Sir Richard Brook, was dealt with. The Clerk explained that Mr Ev nos spornli to think that the terms of the' notice with which the In- spector (Mr Farrington), had served him, to abate a nuisance nen. the Norton Arms, implied that the road was a public one. He had explained the point to Mr Evans, that the terms used were those of the ordinary notice, and that no claim was being made to the road. —T be Clerk also gave notice that a few meeting; ago the Inspector was instructed to serve notice on all owners of hous,s, the sewage whereof drained into any streams in the locality, to abate the same in accordance with the req lirements of the Rivers Pollution Act.—The Council, therefore, decided that Mr Farrington should make an intmection and report on any such places to the committee.

RUNCORN NOTES. NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT].

The annual excursion of the Runcorn and Widnes Co-operative Society took place on Monday last, when a most enjoyable day was spQnt at Blackpool. The'town and the environments Of Blackpool have been so often described that to do so again would be superfluous One's duty is:fulfilled when the fact is recorded that the members who availed them- selves of the opportunity of visiting the favourite seaside resort were quite no to the usual fizure ; and that the " Stores Trip "still taKes the palm for popularity.

The Runcorn Wesleyan Century Fund rear•bes the consummation desired in the completion of the last work undertaken, is, the renovation of the Preston Brook WesleyanlChapel and Sunday School, a state of things whereat the Wesleyan community can well congratulate themselves. Commencing with 'Old Brunswick," Halton-road, Weston Point, and now Preston Brook, the much desired altera- tions have been carried out. On Wednesday the new Sunday schools and class rooms at Pr3ston Brook will be opened by Mrs R H Posnett, subse- quent to which the RAN? T J Choate (Chairman of the Liverpool District) will preach. A capit tl pro- gramme which will render the day a red-letter one in the history of Preston Brook Wesleyaniem, has been arranged.

---- Lastrinrsday, Mr Frank Hignett Speakman, brother of Lance-Corporal E Speakman, of the Run- corn Special Service Volunteer Corps, left Runcorn for Aldershot, prior to his embarkation for service with the South African Field Force. Mr Speakman is well-known throughout the district as a chemist of repute,and when it became generally known that he had decided on joining the Army Medical Corps, it was as generally decided that the Corps would secure an acquisition in Mr Speakman. Amongst other things Mr Speakman is the patentee of a capital" Patent Medicine," which enjoys a fair reputation. General regret is felt throughout the county at losing so capable a chemist.

Mr Hall Caine, vvhoss latest production " The Eternal City "is certainly a gain to English litera- ture, but Rancorn man by birth. One authority has it that his people were " water folk, and one day as Mr and Mrs Came came down to Runcorn in their barges, whilst stopping at Runcorn, the good lady honoured the town so far as to give birth to the now famous Hall Caine." A late lamented Run- corn sige has also given utterance to tke following remark: "If Runcorn can praduce such men as Hall Caine in twenty-four hours (the length of stay of his parents) ; what could it not do in 24 years !

It behoves me to call the attention of local farmers and milk-sellers to a new order of the Board of Agriculture, which comes info operation on the Ist of Septeraber. It is to the effect that milk which contains less than 3 per cent of milk-fat shall be presumed to be not genuine ; and milk which COD- tainq less than 8.5 per cent of milk-solids other than milk-fat (not being sold as skimmed, or separated, or condensed milk), shall also be considered as not genuinefor the purpose of the Sale of Food and Drugs Act, 1875 to 1899; and skimmed m'lk with a less percentage than 9 per cent of milk-solids shall also be considered as not genuine for the purposes of the same Act. Altogether the "Sale of Milk Regula- tions, 1901 "as they are cited, are pertinent, and concise, and aim at securing a genuine article for public consumption. Truly, the days of the "Iron Cow "are numbered, for at length the public are armed with a standard of purity which the Iron Monster" can never attain.

SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

Demonstration at Runcorn.

The Lifeboat Crew Division of the Sons of Temperance, , which ha its home at Runcorn, is a very flourishing branch of a very usetul Friendly Society, and is a branch that deserves well of all temperance people for ' the good work it accomplishes in the town and district. On Saturday tau, the Division had its annual demon- stration. The first part of the proceedings, which took the form of a parade through the principal streets of the town, was carried out with great credit by the several marshalls responsible for it ; and although the weather was dull and a drizzling rain tended to make the attend- ance a small one the muster as very creditable and was witnessed by large crowds. The procession was headed by the Weston Brass Band, and moved in the following order :—Bro Heesom, banner-master ; the banner, borne by Bros C Johnson anti J Cheaters, whilst the guys were controlled by Misses Hfit, L Johnson, L Barker and F Barker; the Juvenile Branch (male and female 93 ; Bros E Hallaworth, P MWP (Salford), Walter Davies, M W C (Manchester) ; the Adult Branch 120 and several gaily bedecked carts. DIA marshals included Bros James Johnson, secretary ; Edward Key, secretary 'juvenile branch, ; D Bisbrown, treasurer ; T Clarko, steward ; T Holt, district secretary ; T White, patron of Cadets; and E Wilkinson. Amongst the visiting biethren were Bros S D Ad, Weston, and T Kniveton, Weston. _

Upon tne return of the procession to the Greeraway- road Schools, a capital sandwich tea was partaken of by all and later a public entertainment and meeting were held. The ladies responsible for the catering were Wsdames Key. Bisbrown, and Helt,and the Misses M A Holt, Marion Holt, F Barker, A Barker and Morefield. The public meeting and entertainment, which com- menced at seven o'clock was fairly-well attended. Mr 11 Biker presided, and he was supported on the plat_ form by Messrs W Davies, MW C. E Hallsworth P WP, T Cecike, MWP, T J Holt, GS, D flis-' brown, M W T Hecieem, WP, J Johnson, FS' Tames White, WP, E Key, SC, E Tomlinson, WC, and others. The meeting opened with a selection by the Weston Brass Band. The Chairman, said although outside the Society, and not a member of the "Sons of Temperance," he was quite in sympathy with them. The two most impor- tant objects of the Society appeared to be, to promote total abstinence, and to promote thrift—both objects indisputably good for the individual, the community, and the country. (Hear, hear) It was very bard to convince people that total abstinence was the best thing for them, especially those people wile said they knew how to take care of themselves; they did not require teaching what was good for them, and could mind their own business. Tney were a hard class tn deal with, and so toe were the mederate drinkers. He contended however, that the liquor traffie was too dangerous to allow for any moderation. (Applause.) Mr James Johnson, secretary, submitted the numeri- cal and financial report of the order. merle up to the 31st December last, which showed 49,287 adult mem- b re, 205,000 juvenile members, being an increase for the year of 2.844 adults, and 2166 juveniles. The total funds were £209,703 or a gain of £13.924 for the year. The capital of the juvenile fund was £11,502 9; 11. He was glad to report that the sickness and death rate of members during the past year had meintained its hitherto, low state. The average rue of sickness was 171 days, and the death rate was only 72) per 1030 1 members per annum. Thi9 was highly satisfactory fir the averarte death rate for the last quarter of a century was but 8 per 1,000 (Hear, hear.) The Runcorn su h- I division had a membership of 323 adults and 130 juveniles, or a total of 453 Toey were. however, 1 anxious to increase that figure, and willing to take members from birth to 45 years of age, (app:serse.) Be hoped that meeting would result in some good to the Temperance cause in Runcorn. (Applause.) Mr Walter Davies, most worthy chaplain of the Order addressed themeetme, on the importance of inculcat- ing the childhood of the nation in thetprinciplea of to-al abstenanee. If they secured the children, in a very few years they would have the support of the country, and would be able to assist in making public opinion. (Hear, hear.) He spoke of the higher gifts that man received from his Creator. Was it then for them to destroy those noble q aalities ? Rather they should use them in helping to raise up a better manhood and ii better wemanhood. As an organisation they took an interest in the public welfare, which was to them a religious duty. (Applause.) Aed they extendei a hearty invitation to these willing to join in furthering the work. (Applause.) Mr E Hells worth, in an eloquent address, said teetotalers were often told they were ton extreme in their requirements for the control of the licpier traffic. They were charged with being narrow, stingy, and niggardly and to!c1 that if they were going in for legislation at all they should modify their views. In so far as it destroyed their iboys and girls; in so far as it created an unnatural appetite for an unnatural stimulant, and in so tar as it sapped the very stamina of the neeion, they could not modify their views, Tney were asked to be not so extreme.'nfle held that view once, but it was altered now. (Hear, hear.) The Brewster Sessions were corn- ing on at Runcorn, and there were to be two applications to put stimulants where the poor people lived. Where: the teetotalers of Rancorn keine to remain idle ? Some people talked of a good publican. He had yet to find one ; for it was not natural, that any of the people, who sold that which tended so much to the destruction of humanity, could be pos- sessed of the attributes of humanity. (Applause.) Be criticised the action of Parliament with regard to the Bill for the protection of children; and alluded to the manner in which publicans had come forward as vatriotsand put into power a Government which from its very composition could not oppose the liquor traffic. Their judges told them that nine-tenths of the cases that came before them were due to the liquor traffic. Were it not for the liquor traffic their slums would not be so great ; their workhouses would not have so many inmates in them ; and they would have a better man- hood and better womanhood. (Hear, hear.) Be deplored the fact that the influence of the street had a tendency to destroy the influence of the home, and appealed to his hearers to help forward the work which would rectify that evil. (Applause,) If they did not, then their lade and lasses would gravitate to the "public" or the " tenements of slumdom." Be had stood outside the prison gates, and seen the love of a child for its mother lost threugh the evils of drink. he had seen too that eduction would not stop drunkenness. Total abstinence did atop it. (Applause.) They as a society were making headway; yet they were not satis- fied with the progress of the Order in Runcorn, there- fore he added his appeal to that of those speakers who bad preceded him. (Applause.) During the evening the following programme of music, teee, was submitted : " Roman Soldier," male voice party (conductor Mr R Yates); " Calvary." Miss Carter; "Britons," the choir; violin solo, Mr J J Stubbs; recitation, Mr F Littler. The accompaniments were played tor Misses Hill, M A Holt and Mrs S Yates. Mr D Bisbrown moved a hearty vote of thanks to Mr Baker for presiding; to the speakers (Messrs Davies and Hallsworth) for their addresses, and to the artists for their excellent items, and to the ladies who had so kindly aseisted with the tea.

MrJT Holt selmded. au i thP vote having ben carried with acclamation was acknowledgd by the Chairman.

ST. HELENS.

THE FREE LIRR&RIES.—THE ANNUAL REPORT. It tbe meeting of St Helens Corporation Free Libraries Committee held on Wednesday, the librarian submitted the annual report, which stated that the libraries and reading-rooms had again been greatly used, though there had been a decrease in the number of issues of books It was gratifying, however, to state that the decrease had been mainly in fiction, the issues of books of science, art, history, biography, and travel showing a considerable increase. The aggregate total issues of books for the year has Peen 170,637, and the computed number of visits to the reading-rooms 564,000. The additions to the stock of books have been larger than usual, owing to the compilation of new catalogues for Sutton and Thatto Heath branch libraries, it being con- sidered desirable to add over; 200 volumes to each of those two branch libraries. The number of volumes aided to all the branches had been 1,446, making a total of 32,254 in the central and branch libraries. The report was adopted, and ordered to be printed.

YOUTHFUL THIEVES.

Coal Dealers' Office Broken Into.

At the Warrington Police Court en Thursday, two small boys named Walter Peplos:, and Henry Banner, both living in Tanner's Lane, were charged with stealing from the premises of Flether Burrows and Ca., Ltd.,Dallam-lane, at 9.50 en Tuesday night, an inkdtand and pen wiper value 2s.— The Chief Constable said the prisoners might hays been charged with breaking into the premises, but considering their youthfulness and the fact that they would have had to be committed to the QoarterSessions,he had reduced the charge to one of larceny. Messrs Fletcher, Burrows and Company's office was broken into on Tuesday night and the place was ransacked. An effort was made to interfere with the safe—a very amateur attempt certainly—and several windows were broken, and it was thought that burglars of a rather more dangerous type than the prisoners had visited the premlees Information waS given to the police, and careful inquiries were made with the result that the boys were apprehended by P C Stewart. Banner made a statement to tbe effect that be was playing in the yard when Peplow asked him to go to Pearson and Knowles' offide and see if they could get in. " There's a lot of money," be added, "and a safe inside." They went to the office but could not get inside. They then went to Fletcher Burrows' office. Peplow smashed a window and got in the offile between the bars, cutting his fingers in the attempt. He (Banner) followed him and they tried to open the safe but could not. They took the inkstand and pen wiper and threw them behind the door, from which place they were recovered by the police. Peplow made a statement which somewhat differed from Banner's but was in effect and substance the same.—Peplow now stated that Banner asked him to go to' Fletcher, Burrows, office as he knew' where al the safes and drawers were and the drawers! were generally unlocked."—lt was stated that Peplow had been an inmate of a truant school for irregular school attendance.—The magistrates ordered the boYs to receive six strokes with the birch and to be detained in the cells for three days.
