PEN KETH AND SAN KEY FLORAL

FETE.

Local horticulturists and bird fanciers took a great deal of interest in the eighth annual show of the Penketh and Sankey Window, Cottage Garden, and Bird S aciety which was held on Saturday, toe exhibits being staged in two large marquees erected in a field opposite to the Infants'School, reakoth. Tbe weather was anything but ideal,pod duricg the greater part of the afternoon and evening it rained steidily,and at times the downpour was. very heavy. This naturally affected the attendance very seriously, and those present during the afternoon were but few in number. In the even- ing the weather improved slightly and there was likewise an improvement in the attendance. Whilst last year the society had a record gate, the takings this year were very small. In the window and cottage garden section an improvement in qttality was noticeable in several classes, whilst in some others there was a marked falling off, though this is more than accounted for by the diffieulties of the season, and the dry weather experienced in June and previously was, by the exhibits, proved to have had bad effects. In point lof numbers tie entries in this section were about equal te those of last year, though, if anything, there was a slight increase in the number. In the bird section, in which competitors were admitted from any point In the tea mile radius of Penketh, the con- petition was keener and the quality was considerably , superior, when a comparison is made with last year's' birds, whilst there was also a numerical advantage of '3O in the entries. The offer of a challenge cup for the first time also served to increase the competition. This is a handsome trophy, purchased by the club by sub- scription,. wnicn will become the property of any corn • petitor winning it for three ceusecutive yes rs or on five distinct occasions, This was well won by Mr J Harnpson who showed no less than 15 birds,and won the cup with a very beautiful, evenly marked, yellow Yorkshire can- ary which was in splendid condition. In tact it was the admirable conditien of the bird that caused the judge to decide in its favoue, rather than for a very free crest bird which was shown by Mr R Jenkins. Two I exhibits appearing among the British birds which attracted much attention were two nightingales. In the cut flower classes, roses and dahlias were not by any ,1 means strong, and two tempting first vrizes, offered ' respectively by Miss Bolton and Mr J B Aitken, were not awarded on account of lack of competition and the mediocre quality of the few specimens entered. The strongest clesses were undoubtedly the sweet peaa, phlox and hollyhocks, but more particularly the former two. In the vegetable classes, marrows made the best show, whilst a very fair standard in fruit was maintainel, Bread-making drew a number of competitors and was undeniably one of the most useful of the classes. There were a number of es non.competitive exhibits, and unique atneng these was a fine branch of growing, and consequently, green almonds, grown and exhibited by Mr J E /gerly of Ponketn: A fine collection of cut fl owers, amongst which were, asters, phlox, sweet peas, stocks, peppiss, Sec., and a quantity of cameo apples, Was put in by Mr B Aitken, Peaketh, whilst a similar collection of phlox, roses, a seat peas (notably some very pretty heliotrope bloo:ns), plc otees and carnations,was srfewo by Mr George Clarke. Penketh, Mr W Harrabin, Widnes, exhibited some splendidly grown cucumbers and tomatoes. Mr R Garnett, Hall Nook, Peoketh ' also showed a good c ollection of cut flowers, whilst a collection of finely grown vegetables, fruit and ed I were was exhibited by Mr W E Brown as the products of 'the gardens of the Friends' Scion!, Penketh. Amen other " not for competition " exhibits were those by Mr Ei Sampson, who put in a number of well grown red cabbages ; Mr C E Parksr, wno showed; some coeice apples, and Mr James Parnell, who appears te be a suc- cessful grower of beetroots and marigolds. The judeing appeared to be carefully and well done, this work having been Oct into the capable hands of tee following :—Mr G Clarke, L71711M, for the horticultural section ; Mr A G Davies, Manchester, for the bird section ; and Mrs Picktoa, Penken. for bread. ' Toe work of o rgaimatien largely devolved upon the two secretaries, Mr George Clarke, Great Sankey ; and NO E R Godwin, Peaketb, the latter being assistant secretary. A very large share of the work has this year fallen upon Mr Godwin. Mr Clarks having owing to necessities of health, had to take a three weeks' holiday, from which he only returned a day or two 'previous to the event. However, he gave the com- mittee and his assistant full advantage of his poet ex- perience, this assistance being of a very useful char- acter. The society,thoh-gn a small one,hee also a strong list of vice-presidents, a pleasiog balance sheet, and a good subscription list. The Peaketh Tannery Brass Band (conductor, Mr J Harper; bandmaster, Mr J Fioce) was in attendance arid rendered many excellent selections during bl:n the afternoon and evening. The programme for the after- noon was as Quick march, "roe Humber"; selection, "Gems of Eoirgreea Melody;" vats% e ile wild;" selection, "Songs of Scotland;" sehettiscee, "The Belle of Coonville;" selection, "Orispino;" pelca, "S on- I beam."

The Show was declare I open during the course cf the afternoon b 7 the President, Mr J B Aitken, who was accompanied by Mrs Aitken, The particular theme ol his remarks was a strong appeal to working men to go in for gardening, which they would find a pleasant and interesting oczupation. A vote of thanks was unanimously accorded to the President on the mot.on of Mrs tt Garnett. seconded by Mr G Thompson. Kindly references were also made to the work of the secretaries and a vote of thanks was accorded by acclamation on the suggestion of Mr Garnett _ .

In the evening the challenge cup was presented to the soccessful eorhp,titor, who was very heartily congratu- lated, by Mrs J B Aitken. Sudden Death of a Labourer. Shortly after noon on Tuesday, a labourer named Edward Hough, 33 years of age, was proceeding along Rylands-street, to his lodgings at 8, Friars Green. when he was observed to fall to the ground. A young man called the attention of P.C. Cooper, who found Hough lying on his back. Dr. Kisby nappened to come 1113011 the scene and pronounced life to be extinct. The deceased bad for some time suffered from an affection of the heart, and it was [ not deemed necessary to hold an inquest.

EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

CHURCH PARADE OF YEOMANRY AND VOLUN- TEERS AT NEWTON.

The annual church parade of C Troop of the Lancashire Hussars, together with a contingent of the 6h V.B. King's Liverpool Regiment, took place on Sunday afternoon. With the band, under the leadership ot Bandmaster Batley. the Yeomanry paraded 104 strong. The detachment of the Volunteers numbered 40; and were under the command of Colour-Sergeant Appleton.An interesting service was held at St Peter's, and the Vicar (the Rev J Ryder) preached a most eloquent sermon. A Newton Man Rescued from Drowning. Some excitement was caused at the aorta end of Prince's landing stage Liverpool on Monday after- noon through a man named Donald 'l'lntyre,of New ton-le-Willows, jumping into the Mersey. Corran and Chamberlain were loudly cheered by the scores of persons who from the Landing-stage watched their plucky endeavours to save tie man. The German Bottle Trade Dispute.—St. Helens Delegates. The dispute between the glass bottlemakers of Germany and their employers has aroused great interest in the glass ;;ottlemaking centre of St. , Helens and other districts of England where the trade is carried on. For some years there has been a keen competition between the Eng- lish bottlemakers and their foreign—mainly German—rivals' and the present dispute is tck be discussed at an international conference of glassworkers, to be opened at Hanover on Sunday next. It is undentood that the German workmen are contending for the right of combination and increase' wages, and they are being financially supported bj their English fellcw-workers. The British delegates to toe international conference will be Mr ft Hunter, of St. Helens, secretary ; Mr W Hale, representing the Lancashire Glass Bottle- makers' Association ; Mr A Greenwood, secretary, and Mr W %neater, representing Yorkshire ; Mr Heptenstall and Mr Robinson, representing the North of England ; and Mr Murray, representing Ireland, with Mr T Voelkel, acting as interpreter The delegates left Englani on Friday.

Great Loss of Life.

The Skazway steamer Islander, belonging to the Canadian Pacific Aliv.kin line, struck an iceberg near Jainceu. Alaska, on Friday morning, and foundered. GRAPHIC DETAILS OF THE DISASTER.

- A grapoic acnant ot toe disaster is telegrApbed by the Victori% correspondent of tlo Lmidoa " Erening News." He ea7o _ .

There Was £475,000 in gold, which was being brought from Itiondike, on cue Islander, £lOO,OOO ot watch was carried by passengers. Mr H. H. Hart, who spent 16 years in the bleak Klondike as a gold miner, lcia;. £135,000 in dust. So ssvere was the siock when the Islander struck the iceberg that every door was jammed fast in the staterooms, and pasaengers and crew were forced to break thrrmgh windows to reach the deck. :steward Simpson lost ten men in his department, and his description of the wreck is very clearly given. Awakened by the shock, be broke out of his room and reached the bridge, where the pilot, La Blond, was on the watch, and with his mate ordered out the lifeboats, as the vessel was then filling rapidly, and the chief en- gineer reported that the pumps were unable to take care r..t the water. He then hurried below, and with an axe . broke down the lower stateroom doors, and continued the work of liberating the imprisoned pasaen,gers until the water forced him to go to the upper deck. By this time the ship's boats wer..l loaded and bad get away. While the officers were getting out the last life raft Captain Foote called to all han..ls to clear the ship. as she was about To go down. This was the captain's last order, as at that moment the ship sank. When the ves- sel was going down her barters exploded, causing the death oi many who might otherwise have escaped. Tee captain, leaping clear of the wreck, was picked up by the lifeboat, which in some manner was overturned. Some nasamgers say that Captain Foote reached the lifeboat. hut that when be saw the extent of the disaster he jumped overboard. piny Di passengers, beaded by the I dander's chief enginser, reached Treadwell, A 14,111, at nail-past eight in the morning, having walked up the beach twenty- eve miles to appeal for help. Toe steamers Lucy and Flossie reaponded promptly. The Flo9Sie returned from the scene of the wreck at noon, with her flag at half- maw:, baying the surviving pas;engers and six of the dead aboard. A description of the disaster by the cur- is heartrendinar, and the unanimous testimony is thst all were heroes and none cowards. Pilot Ls Bond, who had charLre of the steamer at the trine she struck, says ;—" The night was line, and as we always expect to m it ice a sharp look-out was kept. About 2 43. when the crash Cam., the stearn=r was under lull speed. No ice was in sight, and there was no fog. The fatal berg was no dcub: even with the water. After she s'ru:ik we stopped the engines, when Captain Foote appeared wi,h the night watchinao,who reported the ship leaking for ward. I told Captain Foote we bad hotter head for the b.:.ach, but the ship was taking water so fast she could not answer her nelm. Then I called the mite, and oirdered tne boats out. This was done, and they were loaded with passenger'. Many passengers jumpa-d overboard, with life pre-servers on. I jurnpel overboard, and was in the water two hours and a (patter before securing a piece of wreckage." Warrington Beaten by St Helens.

Warrington sustained defeat on Tuesday evering when they met St. Helens in a First Division Lague match. The engagement took place in the S. Helens Corporation Baths, both the teams being representative. In the first half Warrington defended the deep end,and generally got the ball first from the swim-off, but St. Helens played a superior game. 'fhe St. Helens cus- todian was tested in the first minute, but fisted away and from a foul Penninnton passed to Walker, who opened the score for St. Helens. Some lively play followed, and Heaton added the atcond for St. Helens from a foul. Ashton missed by inches with a fine shot at the S. Helens god, and after Lomax had made a gond attempt at the other end he succeeded iu peen-trat leg the Warrington defence, after a good pass by renn ington. Th- 5. Helen; defence WAR now tested, but it was up to the mark, and half-time arrived with St Helens leading by three vale to nil. On resuming, a foul was granted against St. Helens, and Peers mulled a fi •e chance. Several shots at the St. Helens goal were over, but last Peers got one through with a back- handed shot. S. Helens were not long in increasing their lead, Lomax scoring after some excellent passing. After tkis the play was even, each team scoring one goal the final being St: Helens five goals, Warliogton two goals.

No new issue is to be made of the 411 postage stamp and as stocka in the hands of postmasters are almost exhausted the stamp will in • few weeks be a thing of of tee past. Since On introduction of the preen parea most rates in 1897 there has been practically no need for a stamp of that denomination.

The Electric Express Railway. The King on Saturday gave his assent to the Mono-rail Bill, which thereby formally, as well as actually. became law. The Bill has been before Parliament two sessions. The tirst session it did not. go beyond the Committee stage of the House of Commons; the present session it passed both Lords and Commons, and altogether has been 38 days in Committee. There has been no such Parliamentary fight over a railway scheme since the first steam Railway Bill was introduced in the forties of th e nineteenth century. The cost has necessarily been enlrmous—in thp case of the promoters alone over £50,000. The next step in regard to the Mono-rail Bill will be to acquire the land on which the line must be laid between Manchester and Liverpool. The promoters have obtained compulsory powers for the purpose, so that in case a landowner asks a fancy price he may be compelled to sell at the ' market rate. No steps have yet been taken in that direction. No difficulty is anticipated in complying with the rather exacting conditions of the Board of Trade as to the working of the line, and in about three years, it is expected, the lightning express will be running between Manchester and Liverpool.

I QUEER CURES FOR DISEASE.

-- Freezing, bahing, illuminating, torturing, frightening and bruising are among the accepted way* 01 curing certain diseases.

BAKING AND FREEZING.

When one has well-developea rueumatistn he is placed in a species of stove and the crystals of uric aci are literally melted out of his body. 'raja treatment is to-day used iu typhoid fever cases.

For various forms cf hysteria the "shocking " system has been introduced. The phrase is misleading. There is nothing morally objec-ionable in the " shocking " system aria, it frequently works remarkable cures. A byeterical ' patient is brought to a doctor who believes in this cure, and suddenly, on the first appear- ance of hysterical sympt uns, the p atient is startled, either by a sudden apparition of some sort or by a load, . startling noise.

This " surprise " rnethcd has cured many persona not only of hysteria but of deafness and stuttering. THE POLLUTION OF THE SHIP

CANAL.

The Case for Manchester.

The Rivera Committee ot the City Council have pre- pared an answer to the charge that Manchester was offending more and more in ad ling to the po:lwien of the Ship Canal. has been allezed again and again at MPA'tines of the Msrsey and Irwell Joust Rivers Core- mittse, who are the local amthority in the matter of river pollution, that tee rtfl lent from the Corpsration Swage Works at D snyhulme had steadily grown worse during the last few years, and that the C -rperation were spending less upon chemic sla new than forme sly. Tnese complaints having been formally brought uncier the notice of the Rivers Committee of the Corporation, Mr Gilbert Fowler, chemist and super intendent of the Davyhuime works, was iestructed to make a report on the subject. This he has now done, and his report was under consideration at the tw•eticia of the Rivers Cmocnittee on Monday. Mr Fowler ststee:— Tne chemist is anxious to do all that is possible to mitigate the intolerable stench from th. Ship Canal in hot weather, from which he is himself a sufferer. From the evidence he has brought forward, however, he does not consider that any further addition of the present chemicals will pereeptibly increase the purity of the effluent from the works of the Manchester Corporation. The chemist to the Mersey and Irwell Joint Coaern.ttee has himself pointed out the danger of adding excessive amounts of chemicals to the sewage, and so increasing the amount of oxide of iron in solution, which after wards rends to sepsrate out as sulphide. At present ' six or seven tacks are in actual operation every day, 11 which is double the number in use when the flow SPAS Ismaller in amount. The chemist does not consider, I therefore' that the tank space is inadequate. There is, in fact, adanger of using too many tanks when work- ing on the continuous flow system, in that during :he hours when the flow is at its lowest the rate of flow through is so slow that the tanks readily tend to oecnine black and septic. 'To work by the method of stationary subsidence would mean the entire recon- struction of the works, and it is very doubtful whether any real benefit would be obtained. At present one or more tanks are emptied and sludged almost every day, and this can be done so long as the steamer is regularly working Possibly some Small improvement m.ght result if pressing were at once resorted to on every occasion when the steamer was stopped for repairs. There has never in the past, how ever, been a greater accumulation of sludge than she could readily overtake. Tee chemist had already re- ported en the pr•eticability of adding deoderisiog chemicals to the lents. This could be done at the cost of about £3,000, but unfortunately tho state of tee canal above the lalincnester outfall is such that any , good effect thus produced would be immediately nullifi-cl as soon as the effident entered the canal. The difficulty in adding oxidising agents, such as magnate of soda and sulphuric ad 5, is that much of the effect would be expended on substanees themselves harmless. It has been calmilated that to perceptibly improve the f filaent for 100 dare in the summer by this means would cost at least £30,000. Toe most practical suggestion appears to be that the work of constructing the new filter beds ahou'd be pushed on as fast as pois eible, especially the steams beds both on the east and west side of the efflaent carrier. These beds, requiring little concrete work and machinery, can be quickly con- structed, and can be used for purifying the present effluent, without any alteration of tanks. Instructions have already been given to put in hand 13 acres on the east secle, and the chemist would suggest that permission be obtained from the L'cal Govern- ment Board to ' cons'ruct, at any rate, as many of those on the west side as come within the boundary of the present estate* At present the land filters en wWch these would be placed are out of action in order to allow levelling operations to be carried out and the ground to become thoroughly dry and consoli- dated before construction work is begun. With twenty. six acres of storm beds and six acres of ordinary bac- teria beds,- about half the present dry weather flow could be dealt with and a very considerable improve- na•nt made in the efflusot. It meet be emphasised, how- ever, that:much of the nuisance from the Ship Canal is produced above Barnton Locks, and will he unaffected by any improvement in the Manchester effldent." The Committee adopted the report, and resolved, that A.Ppa should be immediately taken to give effect to,Mr Fowler'a recommendations. This will involve the crea- tion of about fourteen acres of filter beds, in addition to the thirteen referred to in the report.

A short time ago (says the ss Wilts County Mirror "), Mrs E Feltham, the wile of a young dairyman, living at Abbott:3 Ann, near Andover. was surprised to hear that she had been reported dead. A reporter who secured an interview with Mrs Feltham. states that the case presents some remarkable features. Mrs Feltham is a bright, intelligent young woman of about twenty five years of 'se. She says she can well understand the report. " Toe facts of the case,' she added, " are these. About eighteen months ago I was very ill, and no one seemed to think I thouid ever get better, I had sickening pains below the heart and frequent fainting fits. The doctor told me that my heart and liver were affected by indi- gestion. and I became so weak that my husband had to help me up and down stairs. I us.d to faint away as I sat in a onair by the fireside, and I have sometimes remained in a dead faint for twenty minutes. " Do you bel any pains now ? " the reporter asked. "No, I have no pains, and," with a smile she added, " I sin sure I don't look much like one subject to faint- ing fits." " How (10 you account for the report that you were deed ?" " Well," she said, " I was se ill I never thought I should live myself, and probably other people thought the same." Fainting fits, such as Mrs. Feltham describes, are always.; a serious symptom, and may frequently be traced' to the heart-weakness caused by ar te nia and indigestion. THE DANGER ATTENDING NAVAL

MANOEUVRES.

' *Engineering," referring to the naval mar ce lyre/Leon- tends that in all sham fights—as it u•ed to be the Diablo., a generation ago to style what we now call mar ceuvres —there muss always be abseut the one great element of success and failure in all real fights : the test of aecuracy of gun fire. Let umpires be ever so prescient, they ean never allow for this It is a snare and an advantage ; it makes the task of those who plan the general idea more easy, becau.e it enables an old and comparatively obsolete veosel to be put on an (quality ith one el modern date. That ha plain advantage; the snare,is equally obvious. The practice incline; us robe forgetful of the undoubted defects of many of the shipa upon which we place reliance for the safeguarding cf the country. We are always too apt —perhaps more especially Parliamentarians and Admiralty Boards, are too apt—to gauge our naval strength by a peace standard ; to judge of the efficiency of the Fleet solely by the duties it has barn called upon to perform for nigh upon a nontury, with very few exceptions. Since the year 1805,when the veat sea fight was fought off Cape Trafalgar,no serious call has been made upon the British Navy to put forth its might in battle array;and, indeed,the world yet lacks 4 great practical object-lesson of navel warfare under modern conditions of steam armour, rill ,;1 ordnance, and high explosives. Long may it continue to do so! But though peace operations cannot orove gun efficiency, either in regard to the effect of projectiles fired or the handling of the weapons, there is one great element of naval efficiency that can be fairly well tested by naval manoeuvres, and that is sea speed ; and it is a serious consideration that there was no first-class cruiser nn either side with a designed speed on trial so high as 21 knots. In one case, how- ever, a squadron of cruisers stood a good chance of failing to engage on advantageous terms through hav- ing to wait for the slower vessels. Indeed, the effect of speed In the cruiser class would appear to be empha- sised as a result of the operations.

An" escsssive number" 'of microbes were devoured by Londoners last month• The fact is vouched for by such indisputable authorities as Sr W Crooke,' and Pro- fessor Dewar, the officials who analysed daily samples of the water taken from the valleys of the Lea and ther Thames between the 6:11 and the 15th ult. "Oa minute examination of the microbes," continue the experts, " yin could discover none of a pathogenic character.nog did we find the microbs associated With decomposinr animal matter." A coastguard named Murphy. travelling from Dublin to Belfast on Saturday last, under the impression that the train had come to a standstill. walked out of a third-class compartment when the train was travelling at for'y miles an hour, between Portadown and Lurgan, and fell on to a grassy slope. He was only slightly injured.

A NOVEL DEFENCE. Peculiar Case at Warrington.

Colliers Charged with Theft.

At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, before Messrs W H B'ecaly (presiding) and Joshua Ball, two IVigati colliers. named John Peuman. 48, John-street, Wigan, and Edward Prior, 37, Hardy Butts, Wigan' were charged on remand with stealing from the " General Elliott " Hotel, Croft, some time between July 13:h and August 7rh, four billiard balls, value 143, the prop?rty of James Lodge.—Mr Browne appeared on behail of the defendants, and pleaded riot guilty. _ _

- James Lodge, landlord of the " General Ellictt" hotel Croft, stated tbat the lour billiard balls produced were his property. 13e last saw them safe in a basket in a cupboard in the billiard room, he could not say on what date, but he thot.ght about July 25:b. in consequence of information be received trod th 3 police he subse- quently looked flr the balls and found they were missmg. B y Mr B ',woe : The balls were not marked.

Zion Pennington, son of Thomas Pennington,a broker, carrying on business at 46, Mill-gate, Wigan, stated that on Wednesday, the 7.h icst, he was in the shop when the detemlams came in and offered the billiard balls produced for sale. He refused to buy them and subsf quently gave information to t• e police. P.C. Fiddler, of the Wigan County Borough Police force, stated that in consequence of information he re- ceived he apprehended Peoinan at 4 30 on tne 7th inst He had the four billiard balls produced in his pocks t. He told witness that he found them at the back of John-street. Prior followel them to the Police Station and witness locked him up. By Mr Browne ; Toe uefendants had both been of good character previously. P.S. Banks. stationed- at Newton, spoke to receiving the misoners into custody from the Wigan Police. For the defence, the defendant Prior gave evidence and told a somewhat extraordinary story. Ha said he had livAd in Wigan all his life He went to the " General Elliott " Hotel, Croft, on May 25:h, but had not been since. He vi-sited Croft again on July 27th, but , did not go near the " G?neral Elliott." He stayed at the " Joiner's Arms.'' He 'was unaware cf the f,ct, that there was a billiard room at the "General Dijon." Continuing he said that on August 17th, he got up early in the morning to atttnd-to some young pigeons. He threw one out at the track of the house to attract others. The bird flcw towards a mid- den about five yards from the back gate, which belonged to a Mr. Fairhurst, who owned a wAggon.,tte which went to Croft on Wednesday, Saturday and Sunday. Witness had never been in the waggonette. These was a hole in the side of a midden,and tne pigeon flew into it. Witness got hold of the bird and pulled it out of the hole. A quantity of straw also canna out, and then the four billiard balls produced tolled out. Witness picked them up and took r trm into his house. Later 913 tie went to the "Prince of Wales.' hotel, in company with Penman. As they had been out of work for some time they ran short of money, and Prior thereupon told Penman of his find, and suggested that he should sell the billiard balls. With this object in view they went to Mr Pennington's shop, but tailed to dispose of theni. Penman was taken into custody by P C Fiddler. Witness went to the Police Station to claim the return of the billiard balls, and he was locked up. He now swore that he had never b,en near the " General Elliott " Hotel since May 25A1 and be had not stolen the billiard balls.

Penman also gave evidence and said he bed never been to Croft in his life. Fie proceeded to boar out Prior's statement in every c;etall. In answer to Soperintendent Morgan he denied that h 3 told the constable that be found the balls. _ .

Clunchlor Thcmas Crarke, 473;ThreEn—eon-street, Wigan, said he had known the defendants for fifteen years, and he knew nothing against their character. They were honest and straightforward men and witness went bail for them the previous week. By Superintendent Morgan : Warless was a puNican and the defendants were " occasional customers " of his.

Mr Browne then addressed the court and said there wag a very good legal maxim that .• a person who fins articles is entitled to them against the whole world ex- cept the true owner." Be quoted the case of Regina v Vincent in which it was laid down that if lost property was found, without Rny mark upon it, by which the owner could be identified, it was not larceny for the lindsr to appropriate the same to his own use unicss it was found .that he must have known who the owner was at he time he found the property. He sub- mitted that when he found the tilhaid balls, Prior was not hound to inform the police although it might have been more honourable if he had done en. Not knowing the true owner he had a perfect right to sell them. The case was dismissed.

WINDFALL FOR AN ALTRINCHAM RESIDENT. A Fortune of £BO,OOO.

, A windfall of £BO,OOO has come to a young man ! named William Lawrence Welch, aged 22, who for some time had been working fer Mr Elwin Walsor3, iron- monger, of George-street, Altrincharn,aa a driver. On Mc.nday he received a letter from a firm of solicitors acquainting him with the fact of his very considerable fortune. He has had a somewhat romantic career. He was born at Chester, but at the early age of five removed with his parents to Oxton, Birkenhead, at which place, as well as at Liverpool he received his education. His father, who was a captain for the firm of Messrs Hole, shipping merchants, of Liverpool, was drowned at sea when Welch was 13 years old. Toey were left well off, his mother being eadowed wib £4OO a year by her husband. Owing to a difference of religious views with his mother he shortly afterwards left home. His father had wanted him to become a civil engineer, but not being inclined for a position of that kind he, in 1894, joined the navy as a mid- shipman. He rose to the rank of sub-lieutenant, but for earns reason be left the navy in 1897. He then turned his attention to " waiting," and was employed in the St. Ann-street (Liverpool) es'ablishinent of the British Workman Company. This did not entirely agree with his inclinations,and he then took a fancy to horses. Eventually. ahem!: a year ago, be went to Altrincham and commenced for Mr Watson as a driver, which oceu- pation he followed up to the time of hearing of the fortune which has been left him. Asked how he felt when he heard the News,Welch said be tilt as though he was on his head ; he was delighted. He added that the money had been left Irrn by his late father. It was inveared in shares in Elypt, but he had not go3e into detai's yet. Be could not say what thy were, as he had not grasp-d the matter. lii fact he did not under- stand it. He had a friend—a cotton broiler in E..rypt— who knew he late father. That friend had male inquiries about the matter, through his own solicitor, and, to use the exact words of ti e foetal' a'o yr u lz man "It is through him tnat the thing has come to life. although my mother knew about it, but sbe did not mention it to ma. Mr. Welch expres,ed his intention of going to EAypt shortly. He thought of going into business in the shipping line. For the present he was only to receive £7.000, and the remainder is to come to him when he is 25 years of age.

FIREWORKS DISPLAY AT WARRINGTON.

Mr William Parkinson is to be congtatulatei upon the success which attended the fireworks display ar- ranged by him and given on the Warrington Cricket Club's ground at Art)ley on Thursday last week. This form of entertainmem is al ways highly popular with the public and there wai a large gathering of spectators to witness the display. Mr Parkinson showed commendable enterprise in venturing upon the undertaking and all who witnessed the display agreed that it did him very great credit. A most a:tractive programme was submitted, the corn - wencement of the exhibition being signalled by a salvo of aerial maroons from iron mortars which exploded with a loud report The pscent of a large balloon was an interes:ing I feature. Having ranched a considerable height the balloon threw out a 50 feet chain of floating colours, which assumed different hues every few minutes. The grounds were brilliantly illuminated by mas-es of coloured fire and batteries of colouted Romans and streamers, and this produced a very pleasing effect. By far the most attractive item was a set. piece representing the Walker fountain in toll play. This pyrotechnic novelty had been specially de- signed for the occasion by Mr Parkinson and his eons, and prove I to be a faithful representa- tion of the banesome fountain which adorns Warrington's park. it was composed of count- less lanceworks of co oured fires and the water was represented by brilliant sprays of lire. Anotherinteresting set-piece was that de- picting a battle between 'two men-of-war. here were seversl amusing mechanical devices, including "The Stupid Donkey," "The Dancing Sailor," and "The Gymnastic Mnnkey." Nlent ion must be made of the numerous fights of rockets, many of which emitted bright coloured stars. The "Fiery Whirl. winds " which ascended in spiral form, proved a pleasing diversion as did els) the silver fountain, which commenced with a revolving circle of fire and suddenly chang-id into a triple revolving foun- tain of brilliant coloured fi,e.—The Volunteer Band was in attendance, and played a splendid selection of music with its accustomed skill.

WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION

An Enjoyable Picnic.

Those whose good fortune it was participate in this year's annual picnic in connection with the Warrington Women's Liberal Association will retain pleasant remembrances of the event. The outing took place on Thursday last week, and not a single untoward incident occurred to mar the enjoyment of the party. The pretty village of Bollingtou,near Dun- ham Park,was the place selected as the venue of th picnic, and it was agreed by all that the selection was a wise one. Tire weather conditions during the morning were not such as to indicate an enjoy- able outing in the afternoon.rain falling at frequent intervals. At noon, however, matters took a com- plete change tor the better and the greater part of the afternoon was brilliantly fine. About 40 mem- bers and friends took part in ' the picnic, and they included the Mayoress and Miss Roberts, Mrs Armitage (president). Mrs W andbMis3 Roberts. Mrs . Clay, Mrs Clegg, Mr and Mrs Mounfield and Mrs Cook. A start was made from the Liberal Club at 12 30 and the drive through the beautiful country was thoroughly enjoyed. The route taken was through Grappeu- hall, High Legh. Hon Green and Mere. On arrival at Bollington the party indulged in a ramble through Dunham forest and afterwards sat down to an excellent knife and fork tea, which was served at the " Old Swan" Hotel. At the conclu- sion of the repast, Mrs Armitage delivered a short speech, during the course of which she remarked thet the picnic had been a most enjoyable one although they had had only a small party. Per- haps the reason for this was the unsettled state of the weather in the morning, and in addition some members were away on their holidays. Neverthe- less the outing had been highly enjoyable.—Mr Mounfield proposed a vote of tnauks to Mrs Armi- tage for the ability with which she had fulfilled tbe duties of President of the Association. He , remarked that she had made many sacrifires in order to further the interests of tee Asiociation.— Mrs Clay seconded and the motion was heartily agreed to.—The party now adj ourned to the bowl- ing green where bowls were indulged in, several ladies proving themselves to be no mean exponents of the game. Rounders and other amusements were also participated in. The return journey was commenced at seven o'clock, the route 'neing via Lymm. Warrington was reached at 8 30'alter an eminently successful outing.

HORSE TRANSACTION AND ITS. SEQUEL.

Local County Court Case.

At the Warrington County Court on Thuraday,last week belore His flobour Judge Bowen Rowlands K.O Edward flawes, farmer, Crowley Ball, Arley, North- wich,claimed £6 from Mr William Cannor,contract- or, Warriagtou, as cue unpaid amount of aifference in the exchange of two horses.—Defendant counter-claimed from the plaintiff the return of a horse.—Mr A Browne appeared for the plaintiff and the defendant was represented by Mr E A Connor.—Mr Browne. in opening the case,said,that Ntr Rawes occasionally traded in horses,aad in May the defendant went to his farm and asked if he had a horse for sale. Plaintiff had tbree, but they were , not quite suitable, and then he statea that there was another horse of his at Mr Cottom's yard, King-street, Warrington. Plaintiff and defendant went to the yard, and the result of the visit was that there was an agreement to exchange horses, the plaintiff to receive £5, the advantage of the exchange, since tbe horse he received was admit- tedly a "shirerer." There was also an agreement that the defendant s:iould have one day's trial of plaintiff's horse and that if found to be suitable the bargain would be considered concluded. A data four days later was fixed for t he plaintiff to receive th a .£5 but as he was no.. in town that day he did not go to Mr Connor's office. Plaintiff sold def aed- ant's horse for £l7 17s as a" shiverer" at Crewe and when he met defendant's father, some time afterwards it was stated that the other horse was was no: heavy enough. Plaintiff offered' to arrange another erThange if possible. Before this further exchange became possible by the plaintiff coming into possession of a heavier animal, the horse originally supplied to Mr Connor fell ill and died. Tney now claimed that the defendant re- taining possession and making no complaint im- mediately after the exchange, concluded the bar- gain and that the £5 was now due. For the defence it was contended that plaintiff had not carried Out fits part of the agreement The defendant agreed to give plaintiff another horse • and £5 for the horse plaintiff offered, pro- vided that the. plaintiff's horse proved suitable and an appointment was made for the plaintiff to see defendant on the fo'lawiug Wednesday to hear whether the horse was right and if so receive his £5. Piaintiff did not turn up but he was told two' days late that his aortae was not suitable and he offered to arrange a second exchange. This he did not do at once taough some time later be told the defendant that he bad a heavier horse for which be wanted'. £l2 in exchange. The death of the horse provided to defendant prevented the conclu- sion of the bargain. The 3 counter claimed for the return of the " shiverer." in cross-examinatiou defendant admitted that a day's trial would he sufficient to decide whether the horse was suitable or not but he understood the agreement was for three nays' trial. On the suggestion of the Judge, a compromise was arrived at, Mr Connor agreeing to pay the plaintiff £l3 10s for the horse described as a shiverrr." On this condition both claim and counterclaim were withdrawn.

Ettttrs to the Otittor.

Land Value Taxation. Sir,—The assessment of land alone accordiag to value regardless of any buildings or other improvements thereon for purely local purposes in towns is one part of the recommendatione in the chief minority report of the Royal Commission on Local Taxation. The inequity, and it might be added, iniquity—of the present system of assessment of land anu buildiags together is well brought out in that report. The Commissioners high government cffitials and members of the present government, submit that the present system affords no true measure of the services rendered or available for each site respectively from the expenditure of common funds. On the contrary, it is poihted out that those who build and improve the most are made to pay the heaviest contribution to !,lerti rates, if, for the time being, land is put to no use then no rate is paid ; if to a little use a small rate only becomes payable—the owner all the time laughing in his sleeve—the value of his partly-used or it may be wholly vacant site continually mounting by growth of population and public expendi- ture, the neighbours meanwhile being saddled with all the burden. Can any system of rating be more absurd, it ore cal- culated to directly encourage land speculators and care- less laud proprietors to withhold land from use for years until it reaches the highest prices land hunger can create ? Building value and not land value bears the chief burden of the rates on the outskirts of towns, the rateable value being perhaps made up cf five parts out of six of building value. This is a most unfaie differen- tial treatment of the suburbs compared with the centres of large towns where the building va'ue does not form nearly so large a proportion of the total annual value. It is obvious that in the most central partiens of towns the value ot the lard enters to a very consider- able extent into the total annual value, being perhaps two parts out of three. The value of land arises in such a way—it is so clearly a social product—as to make it a peculiarly suitable subject for special taxation well able to be borne. The present system of rating operates as a fine upon industry and improvement. The effect is the same as a physical obstacle to the exten- sion of a town. By adding to the cost of production of a further supply of buses, reut becomes dearer every- where and people crowd closer together. Aleessment reform is the only real solution of the housing question: Many people have an impression that the assessment and collection of a land tax will disturb existing con- tracts between landlord and tenant and confiscate part of the rents reserved under current lemma and agree- ments. Those who think so will be agreeably surprised to find they have been all along nursing a delusion. I would gladly eend (1) Memorandum on Assessment Report which deals with flee question of existing ground rents. (2) Copy of the report presented to his Majesty the King by Judge O'Connor, H. C., a member of the Royal Commission referred to above and (3) pamphlet on the question "Can a land tax be shiftea ? " by Henry George to any et your readers sending address on a postcard. The subject is vitally important to rate- payers and to elected persons, and candidates for parish district and Borough Council?, Boar di of Guardians fm. —Yours truly

F. BATT 5, Cholnaeley Villas, Highgate, London N.

===== 0002601_19010823_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERNATIONAL CRICKET. Wanted—Z—Better Team.

Chances of the Proposea Australian Eleven in

the Test Matches.

A County Cricketer writes in the " Daily Express" In selecting his eleven for Australia. or rather, I should say. in selecting his thirteen for Australia, Mr A C 2dcLaren has had to contend with many difficulties of a very serious nature. Even supposing that Mr McLaren had the whole personnel of English Cricket to select from he would *till be beset with endless difficulties in allotting the last half-dozen places. In no case could he escape adverse criticism.

As matters stand be will be accompanied to Australia not by the cricketers whom he desites to take, but merely by a selection of those whe are able and willing ta go.

_ Of the side so far as it has been made up, only six members would probably find a place on present form in an all-England combination. The two best batsmen and the two best bowlers of the current season are none of them included among Mr MacLaren's companions. THE MEN WHO STAY BEHIND. I cannot but think that the first four choices for Eng- land, were the cricketing talent of the whole country available,would probably be C B Fry, K S Ranjitsinhji, Hirst, and Rhodes. No one of these cricketers is avail- able. I might add a fifth choice—J It Mason, whom many people are inclined to put at the nead ot the list. It is, however, the loss of Hirst and Rhodes which makes, perhaps, the most serious hitch in Mr Mac- Laren's plans.

THE PRESENT TEAM. gßut there is no use in speculating upon the impossible or in going any further into the merits of those who cannot, owing to the force of circumstances, sail for Australia.

The team, as far as has hen selected at nres'nt, is made up as follows :—A C MacLaren, G L Jenson, A 0 Jones, H Ii Garnett, Hayward, Tyidesley, W G Quairs, Lilley, Braund, Blythe, and John Gunn. It is a good, even an excellent eleven, but I am not sure that it could beat Yorkshire on their form at the beginning of the month twice out of three times. And this should not be the ease with an eleven which is to uphold the honour of England. The point we have to consider is : Will nig team which is going to Australia be able to keep up the prestige of English cricket? Will it have any chance of SUCCrS3 in representative matches? It will play, I pre- sume, five test matches. To he frank, Ido not see how with the howlers be has self cted, Mr Ma cLaren eanocts to get Australia out for anything less than a mammoth total.

===== 0002601_19010823_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BEST OR NONE AT ALL.

Thus, while deploring the difficulties attendant upon the enterprise of taking at all a representative English ' team to Australia, Many cricketers are asking whether it is not a case of sending the heat or none at all. The matter resolved itself into the simple question as to whether it is fair that Mr MacLaren's eleven should represEn•: the embattled might of English cricket in a series of test matches, of which, on paper, the result would almost seem a foregone conclusion in favour of Australia. They are, as a rule, the better fielding side ; they will prove, I am afraid, a considerably mere powerful batting side, and they are almoat sure to be, beyond all argument and comparison,a more formidable bowling side. • The last is the weakest spot in Mr MacLaren's eleven. Be has no bowler who, if there were an Australian team at present in Englard, would to-lay he chosen to represent this country in a test match. This being the cafe, 113 it wise to play five test matches when the most sanguine of us acknowledge that in order to get Aus- tralia out for a reasonable score it will be quite necessary for luck to play on our side as twelfth man ? If I might make a suggestion. it would be that the number of individuals to go to Australia should he increased from thirteen or fourteen to sixteen. This would allow frrquent changes to be made in the eleven, and wculd do away with the main objection put for- ward by the county committees that an Australian season, following upon a lone English one, is apt to be very injurious to a bowler's future career. Bv tots expedient, it might be found possible to gather a representative sine, as far, at any rate, RS the professional element is concerned.

TERRIBLE DOMESTIC TRAGEDY.

A Whole Family Killed.

The Prss% Association Plymonth correspondent tele- graphs :.....News reached here early on Thursday morninv ef a terrible tragedy at Saltash. In the early hours of yesterday morning the report of firearms was heard entning from a house there occupied by a man named Mortimer. During the day suspicion war aroused by the fact that no signs of life were apparent in the house and at eleven o'clock at night it was broken into, A frightful spectacle then presented itself, the dead bodies of Mortimer. his wife. two daughters. and two sons being di:covered, It is thought that Mortimer first shot his wife and daughters. and then attacked his sons, and af'serwards turned his weapon upon himself. The Press Association Plymouth correspondent, t.te. reVAlDilirt,, later, SAVA the crime is the worst of its kind that has happened in modern times in the west country. About three o'clock en Wednesday morning some of the inhabitamtg ef the Home Park. Saltash, were awakened and startled by the report cf firearms, which apparently came from the house occupied by Mr Mortimer and family. As the blind, remained down during the day, and in response to calls no one answered the door, euenieion was *reused. and the neighbours communicated v,ith the police authorities, who deemed the case so serious as to warrant a forcible entry. This was accomplished on Wednesday evening, when a horrible sight presented itself. In the front bedroom were found Mrs Mortimer, two little girls, and Mr Mortimer, all dead, having been shot in the head whilst in the tack room were the bodies of two hove, also with nearly the whole of their heads shot off. The father must have shot his wife, then his four children, and finally himself. The rooms were covered with blood, and prssented a most repulsive appearance. Tbe GLEN-SPRY lllsTutc&Y. THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS

Boer Commando Surprised.

Seven Boers Killed, Two Wounded, Twenty-one Captured.

Colonel Barker and Major Pine-Coffin have surprised a commando at Doornberg. Seven Boers were killed, two wounded, and 21 clptured, with 35 rill, 40 horses, and three waggons. Boer Attack on Block. houses.

A Laffan telegram from Capetoswn says --Its

Boers m%de two night attacks on blockhouses near

Naauwpoort. 'The first was 'easily repulsed, but • later same of Commandant Lotter's men boldly attacked a blockhouse five miles north of Naauw- poort• This was the " baptism of file" of the blockhouses. The troops behaved excellently. Armoured trains arrived with blaxims, searchlights, and strong reinforcements of infantry. The search. lights played upon the hills while the Maxims and rifles poured showery of lead into any spot where the Boers could lurk. The enemy were forced to retreat.

LICENSING SESSIONS.

Two New Applications Refused.

The annual licensing meeting for the County Borough of Warriagton was held in the Court Room Irlam-street, on Thursday morning. This year,there were no applicatiens of great importance, and con- sequently there was a dimiaished interest in the proceedings. The licensing committee consisted of the Mayor (Alderman Roberts), Alderman Fran- comb, Mr F Monks, Mr John Geddes, Mr Thomas Birchall, Councillor William fierpoint, and Dr Fox. The three first-named magistrates were absentees, and the chair,was occupied by Mr Thomas Birchall. The following magistrates were also on the bench : —Councillor Smethurst., Messrs J W Hayward, Henry Thornton, and J Rymer Young. The only solicitor present was Mr Arthur Browne who appeared on behalf of Messrs Greenall,Whitley and Company in support of an application to re- build the Cattle Market Inn and also represented Mr B lienyon and Mr Wych in their applications. Amongst those present in Court were Mr Alder- man Bolton, Councillor John Evans, Mr T J Down, Mr J Clarke, Mr Drury, Mr R Isherwood, Mr Gough and 3 Cliffe. THE CHIEF CONSTABLE'S REPORr.

The Chief Constable's annual report which was submitted to the justices read as follows :—I have the honour to submit to you my sixth annual report relative to the busineas of the Brewster Sessions, the period dealt with being the year ended July 31st, 1901. Subsequent to the last extension of the Borough boundaries the population according to the census of 1891, was 55,068. According to the late census it has increased to 64.211. There are in the town, 87 alehouse, 39 beerhouses, and 83 " off. licensed " premises (Including those licensed for the sale of wine or spirits), total 211, a proportion of one license to every 304.45 of the population. Onei licensed victualler (John Taylor, " Unicorn Inn," Winwick-road), was fined .£lO and costs, his licence being endorsed, for permitting drunkenness and one beerhouse-keeper (James Payton, "The Old Blueback," Allen-street),was fined las and costs for a similar offence. The licence of the former house has since been transferred to a new tenant. Some 580 visits of supervision have been paid to licensed premises by inspectors and sergeants dc- tailed for such duty, and 16 licence-holders have been cautioned by these officers for slight irregu- larities. Generally speaking, the licensed houses have been well conducted. Licences have been transferred to 16 licensed victuallers, three beer- house-keepers, and four off-licence holders, making a total of 23 transfers. Last year the number of transfers was 23. Twenty-six alehouses,seven beer. houses, and 13 other public rooms are licensed for music and dancing. At the alehouses and beer- houses so licensed no professional performers are employed. For the performance of stage plays 12 1 places are licensed. During the year the magis- trates have dealt with 265 charges of drunkenness. viz:-192 against male persons and 73 against females. There were 246 convictions and 19 per- sons were cautioned and dismissed. Last year the charges of drunkenness were 302, or 37 in excess of this year's total. Of the pergons charged during the present year, 183 were residents of the town and 82 were persons who had no permanent abode here. I should point out that six persons were convicted three times, and 18 persons twice each during the year, so that 54 of the charges represent only 24 individuals. Thera is as yet no provision in this locality in the shape of a home for inebriates under the Inebriates Act of 1898.

Accompanying the report is a list showing the number of houses, of licensing prosecutions and of charges of drunkenness in the borough during each year of the past decade. In 1892 there .were 88 licensed victuallers, 48 beerhouse keepers and 73 hopkooners as compared with 87 licensed victuallers 39 boathouse keepers and 85 shopkeepers at the present time. In 1892 the charges of drunkenness numbered 680 : 1893, 606; 1894, 458; 1895, 328 ; 1896, 264; 1897, 266; 1899, 418 ; 1900, 302; 1901, 265.

A second table gives a comparative return of the statistics of drunkenness in several other towns and cities, the figures being relative to the same period dealt with in the report% We extract from it the following statistics : _

APPLICATION FOR AN OFF-LICENCE IN MARSH- EIOUSELANE. THE DISADVANTAGES SUFFERED BY THE WORKINGMAN, Mr BROWNE now made application on behalf of William Wych,6B,MarshHouse-lane for a beer off-licence. He said that an application was made last year but the foil facts were not laid before them. On that occasion there was an opposition, which did not appear to-day because one gentleman who was supposed to be oh jecting to it last year never gave any instructions at all to that effect. He had told his (Mr Browne's) client that he never gave any instructions, and knew nothing about the oppasition. That was the way they got opposition to licences. The neighbourhood for which the licence was required was increasing by leaps and bmcds. Since the last application nearly forty new houses bad been built and occupied oppo- site to Mr Wych's shop. During the last tws years the whole of Elaine-street bad been built, and cmtained from 80 to 100 houses. The nearest pubic house was the Cemetery Inn. Last year it was said that the people could go across the field to another public-house in Man chester-road,but it was then pointed out that if they did they were trespaS sing. Now however, they csuld not go across because it had been , built upon. There was no opposition to the granting of the licence from the neighbourhood itself. It was a neighbourhood of respectable houses occupied by work- ingmen. Oa the last occasion it was stated that the I application was made for Messrs Walker's benefit. I His client said there was absolutely no truth in it, and to prove that, he had dis.ociated himself from the solicitors of Messrs Walker and instructed him (Mr Browne) to make the applica- tion. He was not going to tie himself in any way and whatever conditions the bench might lay down in grant- ing the vrtificate would be faithfully carried out. He invited the magistrates to visit the neighbourhood. He was there on the previous day and was astounded to see the number of bailees which had been built. Within 400 yards of the shop 400 new houses had been built within the last three years. Toe Cemetery Inn was at least ten minutes walk, and in the otherrdirection the Albion Hotel, Battersby-lane, was fourteen or fifteen minutes walking distance. If a man waste I his dinner beer, the legislature in its supreme wisdom had forbidden him to send his children for it. He bad to walk about a quarter of an bour to get home from work, and then it he went to a public- house for the beer, this occupied another twenty four minutes.

PRESCOT. Huyton Application. Refused for the Twelfth Time.

At Prescot Licensing Sessions, on Wednesday, an applicatioa was made for a full licence in respect of pre- mises known as the Carters' Arms, Huyton, this being the twelfth time this application had been brought for- ward,—Mr J W Knowles, Widnes, appeared f3r Josep'l Fryer, the applicant, and Mr Humphrey Nelson, barris- ter, Liverpool,opposed on'behalf of the vicars of Huyton and Roby and residents of the district.

, several witnesses were called by Mr Knowles to state that in their opinion the licence was necessary. In his remarks to the magistrates, Mr Nelson that the first application in respect of the Carters' Arms was made in 1892. In the following year the magistrates , were confronted with the fact that the house was com- plete, hut on that cccasion and every year since the bench had refused the application. It seemed that Messrs Burton, Bell, and Co., the owners, wished to make the best of a bed bargain. Such perseverance, be thought. was worthy of a better cause. It was undesirable that tied houses should be encouraged, and it was also an insult to the magistrates, seeing that the circumstances were so different from these of last year, that the ap- plication should Ise made again. The magistrates unanimously refused the licence. An application for an off beer licence was then made, this also being refused. Mr C W Swift,representing Messrs Greenall, Whitley, and Co.,applied for the sanction of the magistrates to alterations they proposed to make to the Black Horse beerhouse, Warrington-road, Rainhill. Ile explained that if the alterations were made the drinking accommo- dation would be lessened, and no more ground would be taken up than was at present occupied..— Ile matter was adjourned,the bench being of opinion that if Messrs Greenall,Whitley,and Co. obtained permission to moder- nise this old house they ought to give up the licence of some other old house.

G. G. Hemingway, chemist, of Eccleston-street, Prescot, and the holder of wholesale spirit and beer licences, applied for additional similar licences in order that he might be enabled to sell in smaller quantities.— He was granted such licences for wines and spirits, but not for beer.

__.---- - Superintendent /Ss xendale reported that in the Prescot part of the division there were 49 licensed victuallers,26 beersellers, and 6 persons holding other licences, the population (19,197) thus giving 234 people to each licence. During the year 3 licensed victuallers had been proceeded against, 1 being ordered to pay costs, while the other cases had been dismissed. For permitting drunkenness one beersellor had been fined 403 and costs. There was an increase of 43 in the number of persons proceeded against for.dronkenness. . . .

- Superintendent Keigtiley's report stated that in the Widnes police division there were 110 licensed houses No licensed person had been proceeded against during the year, and the houses bad been well conducted. The number of persons proceeded against foe offences against the licensing Acts had been 658, against 672 in the previous year, a decrease of 11. There was an average of 292 persons to each licensed house. There was no application for new licences.

EARLESTOWN DISTRICT SCHOOL.

Reports of H.M. Inspectors.

Dr Arthur Handyside, hon treasurer and correspon- dent, has received the following reports from the Board of Education on the secular work of the school for the year 'ended 31A May, 1901. MIXED DEPARTMENT Though changes in the staff have occurred during the year, the discipline and in- struction ,continue strong and the school shows a very satisfactory record of work." 1 INFANT DEPARTMENT 'this school continues to be conducted with excellent results as to both discipline and instruction."

PUPIL TEACTIER3 : John C Rawlings, Selina Moly- neux, Lucy Wakehan, Ethel Bateman, and Alice Green were placed in the first class, and hfab.l Jones in the second, whilst Florence Kenning and Florence Sutton successfully passed the examination for admission as pupil teachers. Reports of the Diocesan Inspector.

The Rev. T. R. Matthews, chairman of the Committee of Manageniient, has received the following reports on Relgious InFtruction given in the school : MixED Scnoor. :—" rho school has been examined after a year's work of much more than ordinary difficulty owing to the changes of the staff and the unavoidably broken instruction which resulted therefrom. The results shew that the strain has been borne in an alto- getheradmirable manner and by skilful organisation the school has suffared far less than might have been expected. Ira Group I. one hundred and fiftv-three children were presented—almost exactly one-third of the total number and an unusually large percentage to be in so high standards. Here the work shows advance on what ;sag found a year ago ; the instruction given has been clear and precise, and the children answered with equal accuracy and intelligence. Out of the rest of the school, Ft mdard fit., which formed Group 111., and included 91 cuildren, deserves special commendation. It is an admirably taught class, full of knowledge and power to apply it and manifesting much eagerness and receptivity. Not only was the syllabus itself well known but much know- ledge was shown outside the specified limits. In the re- maining groups the syllabus has been drawn up with much care and thought and very good work has been presented tor examination. Speaking as a #hole, the difficulties cf the past year have only shewn in a more striking manner the solidity of the basis on which the excellent results of this school are habitually built up." INrANT ScriOoL :—" The opening service with which the examination commenced was an indication, in its reverence and fulness of the completeness of the Religious Instruction given in the school. Group I. This is a particulerly well taught class, one which the thoughtful answers given made it a pleasure to examine. Old and New Testament narratives have been admirably illustrated frcm the Bible text and the teaching has been such that the children can readily adapt the knowledge they have acquired from their lessons to the receiving of new im- pre•sions in which they can discriminate between right and wrong. Group ll.—flere the work rises to a point of merit beyond that of children of similar age in most schools. Toe lessons are given not simply to inculcate facts but to instil lessons helpful to the little ones in their daily lives. Group lll.—This class consists of the babies. They can answer questions and repeat com- mandments and passages with an intelligence far in advance of their years. Thee have been manifestly taught with much sympathy and capital methods have been adopted." NEW DEPARTCrRE: The boys attending the schools are adopting a distinctive School Cap, with Fladee. It is hoped this will tend to cultivate Esprit de Corps amongst the lads and be otherwise beneficiA in still fur - ther raising the tone of the School. The girls will be at liberty to wear the badge as well.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. The meeting of this Society on Sunday afternoon was presided over by Mr David Owen, in the absence of Councillor William Roberts. The speaker for the occa- sion was Mr William Silcock, who gave an excellent and much-appreciated address on " The Man in the Street." Mr REI Pearson contributed a violin solo with his accustomed ability. The service was also taken part in by Mr Farrar (hon. secretary) and Mr Joseph Hawthorn. Bold-street Wesleyan Church. The preacher at the above place of 'worship on Sun- day morning was the Rev T Bishop, whilst in the arm- ing the Rev C Ell Hocken occuoieu the pulpit. At the latter service the choir rendered the anthem " My sant cloth magnify the 1,,rd." Tt.e collections were on behalf of the Kingswo:d Schools for ministers' sons.
